          "anchor": "stmt-lem-micro-contractions",
          "exact_quote": "Suppose the edge matrices have uniformly bounded operator norms and that the traces and paths in <a href=\"#eq:micro-spectral-words\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:micro-spectral-words\">[eq:micro-spectral-words]</a> converge for every fixed word. For a fixed connected $G$ and fixed continuous vertex functions of polynomial growth, $S_G$ converges in every fixed $L^p$ under the conditional Haar law to a deterministic limit. The same holds for the sum restricted to injective site labels. Products of finitely many such contractions have the products of their limits.*\n\n*The conclusion applies to the contractions defining $\\sum_iw_i(A_a^Q)_i(A_{a'}^{Q'})_i$ at the sampled spin, and to their quadratic contractions with an additional $P_q$ edge, for $q>0$ fixed. In the latter case the limit tends to zero as $q\\downarrow0$ if that edge is replaced by $P_q-P_{\\rm mark}$, where $P_{\\rm mark}$ projects onto the marked spectral axes.",

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-micro-weighted-recursion",
          "exact_quote": "Fix $K<\\infty$. Suppose that $W$ is symmetric, has zero diagonal, and obeys $$\\|W\\|\\le K,\\qquad \\epsilon_n:=\\max_{i,j}|W_{ij}|\\le n^{-49/100}.$$ There is $C=C(K)\\ge1$ such that, for each fixed integer $m\\ge1$, uniformly in these matrices and in real functions $f$ on $\\{-1,1\\}^n$, $$N_m(Hf)\\le\\sum_{l=1}^{m+2}\n \\bigl(C^{m-l+3}(m+1)^{m-l+2}+o_{n;m}(1)\\bigr)N_l(f).\n \\label{eq:source-35}$$ Here $o_{n;m}(1)$ is deterministic, nonnegative, and tends to zero as $n\\to\\infty$ with $m$ fixed.",

          "anchor": "stmt-cor-micro-moment-density",
          "exact_quote": "Let the matrices and normalized linear functions above vary with $n$, and suppose the moments of the finite measures $$\\omega_{n,a}(B)=\\langle Y_a,H\\mathbf1_B(H)Y_a\\rangle_{L^2(\\mu)},\n \\qquad B\\subset[0,\\infty)\\text{ Borel},$$ converge at every fixed nonnegative integer order. They converge weakly to a finite measure $\\omega$ on $[0,\\infty)$ with those moments. This measure is uniquely determined among measures on $[0,\\infty)$ by its moments, and, for some $c>0$, $$\\int e^{c\\sqrt\\lambda}\\,d\\omega(\\lambda)<\\infty.$$ Polynomials are dense in both $L^2(\\omega)$ and $L^2((1+\\lambda)\\omega+\\delta_0)$.",

For fixed leading indices $a,a'$, polynomials $Q,Q'$, and a bounded continuous cylinder $\\psi$, $$\\mu\\!\\left[\\psi(x)\\sum_iw_i(A_a^Q)_i(A_{a'}^{Q'})_i\\right]\n \\longrightarrow\n \\mathbf 1_{\\{a=a'\\}}\\Pi[\\psi]\\int QQ'\\,d\\omega.\n \\label{eq:source-32}$$ For every $\\varepsilon>0$ one also has $$\\lim_{q\\downarrow0}\\limsup_{n\\to\\infty}\n \\mathbb P\\!\\left(\n  \\mu\\!\\left[\\left\\|P_q(wA_a^Q)-v_a\\int Q\\,d\\omega\\right\\|^2\\right]\n      >\\varepsilon\\right)=0.\n \\label{eq:source-33}$$ Here $wA$ denotes entrywise multiplication. For some $c>0$, $\\int e^{c\\sqrt\\lambda}\\,d\\omega(\\lambda)<\\infty$. The measure is uniquely determined by its moments, and polynomials are dense in $L^2((1+\\lambda)\\omega+\\delta_0)$.",

          "exact_quote": "Let $F$ be a smooth compact cylinder, and let $Q$ be a polynomial with $Q(0)=1$.           "anchor": "stmt-thm-micro-relaxation",
          "exact_quote": "The constant $c_*=\\omega(\\{0\\})$ is deterministic and strictly positive.           "exact_quote": "Let $\\nu_n$ and $\\nu$ be random nonnegative measures on $[0,\\infty)$ such that their integrals against $(1+\\lambda)^{-1}$ are finite almost surely, for every $n$ and for $\\nu$. Suppose the corresponding integrals against $(z+\\lambda)^{-1}$ converge jointly in law for every finite collection of positive $z$, including $z=1$. Then $$\\left(s\\longmapsto\\int e^{-s\\lambda}\\,d\\nu_n(\\lambda)\\right)\n \\ \\Longrightarrow\\\n \\left(s\\longmapsto\\int e^{-s\\lambda}\\,d\\nu(\\lambda)\\right)$$ in $C_{\\rm loc}((0,\\infty))$.",
          "source_id": "PM-227.2"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-spectral-continuity",
      "inputs": [
        "Joint Stieltjes convergence",
        "Finite weighted spectral mass"
      ],
      "method": "Compactify the weighted spectral measures, identify their laws by a determining resolvent algebra, and apply the continuous Laplace map.",
      "output": "Functional Laplace-transform convergence at positive times.",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-functional-spectral"
    },
    {
      "claim_anchor": "stmt-prop-functional-identification",
      "depends_on": [
        "step-trace-universality",
        "step-gaussian-trace-tail",
        "step-spectral-continuity",
        "step-gradient-domain"
      ],

          "anchor": "stmt-prop-functional-identification",
          "exact_quote": "The series <a href=\"#eq:source-2\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:source-2\">[eq:source-2]</a> has a Borel version $g\\mapsto\\mathcal A_g$ with values in $C_{\\rm loc}((0,\\infty))$. For both Gaussian and symmetric-sign entries, $$\\operatorname{Law}(A_{n,J})\\Longrightarrow\n Q_{\\rm crit}=\\operatorname{Law}(\\mathcal A_g),$$ where the same edge law and the same deterministic coefficient $c_*$ are used in <a href=\"#eq:source-2\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:source-2\">[eq:source-2]</a>.",
          "source_id": "PM-227.2"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-functional-identification",
      "inputs": [

        "Conditioned cylinder core"
      ],
      "method": "Construct the measurable limiting spectral measure from countable-core resolvent pairings and apply spectral continuity.",

      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-prop-functional-identification"
    },
    {
      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-functional-shift",

      ],
      "evidence": [

          "exact_quote": "For every fixed $m$ and $h\\in\\mathbb R^m$, $$(T_h)_*(\\mathsf P|_{\\mathcal O_h})\\ll\\mathsf P.$$ In particular, lowering $g_1$ by any fixed positive amount gives a law absolutely continuous with respect to $\\mathsf P$.",
          "source_id": "PM-227.2"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-edge-shifts",

    },

It assigns probability one to nonzero, nonnegative, nonincreasing functions $f$ satisfying $\\lim_{s\\to\\infty}f(s)=0$. The Gaussian and Rademacher models use exactly the normalization in <a href=\"#eq:intro-rescaling\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:intro-rescaling\">[eq:intro-rescaling]</a>.",

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **invariant-common-critical-law**: The stationary rescaled spin autocorrelation has the same functional limiting law for Gaussian and Rademacher couplings, with the stated per-site clock and normalization.
- **invariant-relaxed-energy-coefficient**: The limiting closed gradient energy uses a deterministic strictly positive microscopic coefficient. The lower bound and cylinder recovery sequences use the same coefficient.
- **invariant-gradient-domain**: The Euclidean cylinder gradient is closable; its closed graph is characterized by angular weak identities, and the closed form has only constants in its kernel.
- **invariant-surviving-disorder-randomness**: At every fixed positive time, the limiting autocorrelation is finite and essentially unbounded across disorder samples. Its law is not concentrated on a deterministic curve.
- **invariant-support-versus-decay**: Almost every limiting function decays at large times, while the constant-one function belongs to the closed topological support.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-use-autocorrelation-as-mixing-certificate**: Use the autocorrelation scaling limit as a finite-system stopping certificate for total-variation mixing. -> Convergence of the stationary spin observable supplies neither a finite-system mixing certificate nor a stopping error bound.
- **anti-use-change-model-or-clock**: Transfer the limiting law to biased spins, other coupling distributions, or an arbitrarily normalized update clock. -> The stated model and clock hypotheses no longer establish the same limiting law or time coefficient.
- **anti-use-independent-edge-coordinates**: Replace the conditioned edge equilibrium law by independent Gaussian coordinates. -> Removing the renormalized quadratic constraint changes the equilibrium measure and the domain of the limiting energy.
- **anti-use-unrelaxed-energy-as-time-coefficient**: Use the total microscopic energy measure as the limiting time coefficient. -> Microscopic corrections can vanish in norm while changing the rescaled energy; total mass records unrelaxed energy rather than the effective coefficient.
- **anti-use-deterministic-disorder-curve**: Replace sample-dependent limiting curves by a universal deterministic curve. -> Disorder randomness survives in the limit, and fixed positive-time values are essentially unbounded.
- **anti-use-support-implies-almost-sure-nondecay**: Infer that limiting samples can fail to decay because the constant-one function belongs to closed support. -> Closed support in the local topology does not impose behavior at infinity on almost every sample.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The functional limit is defined by an infinite coordinate autocorrelation series for a closed semigroup on a conditioned edge measure. Its microscopic coefficient is specified through limiting moments and polynomial approximation, rather than a finite terminating construction. The source explicitly states: "All polynomial degrees are fixed before $n$ tends to infinity." Finite-system enumeration or finite coordinate truncation cannot certify this functional universality theorem, identify the infinite-dimensional law, or decide its support and disorder-randomness assertions.

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-critical-zero-field-regime** (hypothesis): The main scaling theorem concerns the zero-field model at critical inverse temperature. Extensions to other temperatures or external fields are not established by this result. Blocked use: ['Apply the critical limiting law to a model with external fields.', 'Transfer the critical normalization to another temperature without a separate theorem.']
- **limit-independent-specified-disorder** (hypothesis): Universality is established for the specified independent Gaussian and uniform sign couplings. It does not establish the same conclusion for dependent, sparse, or heavy-tailed interactions. Blocked use: ['Claim universality for arbitrary coupling distributions.', 'Replace the independent mean-field interactions by a different interaction geometry.']
- **limit-stationary-observable** (formal_scope): The observable averages stationary thermal dynamics. The theorem does not itself identify autocorrelations from an unequilibrated initial spin law or supply a total-variation stopping certificate. Blocked use: ['Interpret a nonstationary trajectory as the stationary autocorrelation in the theorem.', 'Use agreement with the asymptotic autocorrelation as a certified mixing criterion.']
- **limit-per-site-clock** (hypothesis): The normalization uses independent rate-one clocks at every site. A different update schedule requires its own time conversion and justification. Blocked use: ['Treat a single update as a unit of the stated continuous-time clock.', 'Assume simultaneous spin updates have the same reversible semigroup.']
- **limit-positive-time-local-topology** (edge_case): Functional convergence controls compact intervals of positive rescaled time. It provides neither convergence through the initial time nor uniform convergence over an unbounded time interval. Blocked use: ['Include the initial time in the asserted functional convergence.', 'Exchange the system-size and large-time limits using local convergence alone.']
- **limit-gaussian-intermediate-proof** (hypothesis): The spectral cap, Haar-frame, and microscopic energy arguments are developed under Gaussian disorder. Transfer to sign disorder is through basis-independent traces, rather than direct transfer of the intermediate eigenframe assertions. Blocked use: ['Assert conditional Haar eigenvectors for the sign model from the Gaussian argument.', 'Apply Gaussian observation-path estimates directly to arbitrary sign-model spectral coordinates.']
- **limit-conditioned-edge-constraint** (hypothesis): The edge equilibrium measure retains a renormalized quadratic constraint and requires an admissible auxiliary parameter. Independent Gaussian coordinates are not a substitute for the conditioned law. Blocked use: ['Use the unconditioned product Gaussian measure as the limiting equilibrium law.', 'Choose an auxiliary parameter at or below its spectral pole.']
- **limit-cap-input-order-and-range** (hypothesis): Cap estimates require the stated spectral-volume range, sufficiently small fixed upper cutoffs, bounded normalized fields, and tolerances fixed before the size limit. Replica norm-density comparisons additionally require positive tilted precision and comparable row covariances. Blocked use: ['Apply the cap bounds below their prescribed lower scale or on unrestricted external fields.', 'Let the fixed error tolerances or replica order vary with system size without further estimates.', 'Use tilted norm-density bounds when the tilted precision is not positive definite.']
- **limit-static-list-interpolation** (approximation): The imported static comparisons initially concern prescribed geometric lists. Their extension to all intermediate scales uses projection tails, finer fixed lists, smaller errors, and a decreased upper cutoff. Blocked use: ['Assume listwise comparisons automatically imply uniform comparison over the continuum of scales.', 'Skip the nearby-cap weight and mean comparison when integrating observation identities.']
- **limit-path-versus-all-fields** (formal_scope): The shrinking-annulus statement concerns the random observation path under the specified laws. It is not a uniform shrinking-annulus assertion for every deterministic external field. Blocked use: ['Replace observation-path concentration by an assertion about every field in the cap space.', 'Discard the distinction between biased path probability and ordinary quenched path probability.']
- **limit-unused-frame-conditioning** (hypothesis): The compression estimate averages over unused spectral refinement before intersection with the good-disorder event. Conditioning on that event first does not supply the Haar independence used in the proof. Blocked use: ['Assume the unused frame remains Haar after conditioning on a refinement-dependent event.', 'Use the compression estimate without the parent-child scale relation.']
- **limit-integrated-curvature-and-exceptions** (formal_scope): Positive covariance control is stated at deterministic scales away from activations, with scale-dependent exceptional events and uniform probability bounds. Its integrated version follows by Fubini, rather than by an asserted simultaneous pathwise bound at every scale. Blocked use: ['Replace the deterministic-scale probability statement by a simultaneous exception-free covariance bound.', 'Ignore the integrated exceptional-path cost in deriving posterior variance control.']
- **limit-common-test-event-and-remainder** (hypothesis): The conditional variance inequality uses a common disorder event independent of the test and retains a supremum-norm remainder. The exponentially small remainder is negligible for fixed polynomial size bounds, not for unrestricted growth. Blocked use: ['Drop the supremum-norm remainder for arbitrary rapidly growing tests.', 'Infer uniformity for disorder-dependent variational tests solely from convergence on a countable core.']
- **limit-closed-conditioned-gradient-domain** (hypothesis): The limiting energy uses the closure characterized by angular weak identities on the conditioned measure. The compensated approximation requires positive variances, a divergent variance sum, and a finite squared-variance sum. Blocked use: ['Replace the closed conditioned gradient by an unspecified product-space derivative.', 'Use compensated polynomial density without its variance-summability hypotheses.', 'Assume finite-coordinate equilibrium convergence alone identifies the limiting energy.']
- **limit-smoothing-size-and-energy** (hypothesis): Observation smoothing and slope identification concern the prescribed cap range and sequences with bounded norms and rescaled energies together with a fixed polynomial supremum bound. The weak gradient is identified through subsequence extraction and an outer limit of cap scales. Blocked use: ['Apply slope identification to sequences with uncontrolled energy or supremum growth.', 'Treat a fixed cap smoothing as the exact limiting gradient without the scale limit.']
- **limit-fixed-microscopic-orders** (hypothesis): Microscopic contractions and difference recursions use fixed graph, polynomial, and derivative orders before the system-size limit. The weighted recursion also requires a symmetric zero-diagonal matrix with bounded operator norm and the stated small-entry bound. Blocked use: ['Increase corrector degree with system size using only the fixed-order estimates.', 'Apply the weighted recursion to a matrix violating its operator-norm or entrywise hypotheses.']
- **limit-effective-coefficient-not-total-energy** (nonconstructive): The effective coefficient is the zero atom of a limiting microscopic energy measure. Moment determinacy and positivity do not provide a finite numerical evaluation or a certified stopping rule for locating that atom. Blocked use: ['Use the total microscopic energy mass as the effective dynamical coefficient.', 'Claim a certified coefficient value from finitely many moments without an additional approximation argument.', 'Treat a finite-system zero atom as the limiting effective coefficient.']
- **limit-corrector-recovery-limit-order** (approximation): Cylinder recovery fixes the microscopic polynomial before the finite-size limit and takes the polynomial approximation limit afterward. The source supplies matching limiting energies without an effective tolerance-based choice of corrector and system size. Blocked use: ['Interchange the polynomial and finite-size limits without a uniform estimate.', 'Present the recovery sequence as a finite terminating approximation algorithm.']
- **limit-full-trace-needs-tail-control** (approximation): Fixed-coordinate resolvent convergence does not by itself control the full autocorrelation sum. The proof requires a separate uniform trace-tail estimate in the successive size and coordinate-cutoff limits. Blocked use: ['Infer full trace convergence solely from convergence of a finite coordinate block.', 'Treat a finite coordinate truncation as a certified approximation with a supplied numerical error.']
- **limit-quadratic-feature-first-comparison** (hypothesis): The coupling comparison first transfers vanishing quadratic-feature traces. Their vanishing controls the additional fourth-order sensitivity needed to transfer the spin traces despite differing fourth moments. Blocked use: ['Compare spin traces using moment matching alone while omitting the quadratic-feature control.', 'Generalize the replacement argument to unspecified coupling laws without the required remainder estimates.']
- **limit-weighted-spectral-continuity** (hypothesis): The spectral continuity principle requires nonnegative random measures with finite inverse-linear weighted mass and joint convergence of resolvent integrals at finite collections of positive parameters, including the total weighted mass parameter. Blocked use: ['Replace joint transform convergence by convergence of mean transforms.', 'Use the continuity principle for signed measures or without finite weighted mass.', 'Assert convergence through the initial time from this positive-time principle.']
- **limit-surviving-disorder-randomness** (formal_scope): The limiting law is genuinely sample dependent, with essentially unbounded values at each fixed positive time. A disorder-averaged curve does not identify the distribution of individual sample curves. Blocked use: ['Replace the limiting probability law by a deterministic curve.', 'Infer concentration of sample curves from agreement of disorder averages.']
- **limit-support-not-almost-sure-behavior** (edge_case): Almost-sure decay and closed support membership are different assertions in the local function topology. The constant-one function belongs to closed support even though limiting samples almost surely decay. Blocked use: ['Infer positive probability of the constant-one function from its support membership.', 'Infer nondecay of typical samples from a nondecaying support element.', 'Impose decay at infinity on every element of closed support.']
- **limit-finite-oracle-obstruction** (nonconstructive): The principal result identifies an infinite-dimensional random semigroup observable through asymptotic form, trace, and moment limits. Finite enumeration or simulation can test finite identities but cannot certify these functional universality and support assertions. Blocked use: ['Report a finite numerical experiment as a certificate for the functional limit theorem.', 'Treat finite truncation agreement as a decision of infinite-dimensional support membership.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that the stationary spin autocorrelation of the zero-field Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model at inverse temperature <i>β</i> = 1 has a common functional scaling limit for Gaussian and Rademacher couplings. With rate-one heat-bath clocks at every site, time is scaled by <i>n</i><sup>2/3</sup> and the mean spin autocorrelation is multiplied by <i>n</i><sup>1/3</sup>. The functions converge in law uniformly on compact positive-time intervals. Their limiting law is not a point mass: it retains sample-to-sample randomness, and its functions decay to zero at large times.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

The equilibrium spin autocorrelation measures how much a system remembers its initial configuration after averaging over its stationary dynamics. At the critical temperature of the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model, this memory lives on a time scale that diverges with the number of spins. We determine its limiting random function and prove that Gaussian and Rademacher interactions give the same law on the stated clock.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Model and result

<a id="eq:intro-correlation"></a>
<a id="eq:intro-rescaling"></a>
Let $D$ be either the standard Gaussian distribution or the uniform distribution on $\{-1,1\}$. For $n\ge2$, let $(J_{ij})_{i<j}$ be independent with law $D$, put $J_{ji}=J_{ij}$ and $J_{ii}=0$, and define <a id="eq:intro-gibbs"></a>$$\label{eq:intro-gibbs}
 \pi_{n,J}(\sigma)=\frac1{Z_{n,J}}
 \exp\left(\frac1{\sqrt n}\sum_{i<j}J_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j\right),
 \qquad \sigma\in\{-1,1\}^n.$$ This is the zero-field model at inverse temperature one. Each site has an independent rate-one clock. When the clock at site $i$ rings, its spin is resampled from its conditional law under $\pi_{n,J}$: $$\mathbb P\{\sigma_i\gets a\mid\sigma_{-i}\}
 =\frac{\exp(a h_i(\sigma))}{2\cosh h_i(\sigma)},
 \qquad h_i(\sigma)=\frac1{\sqrt n}\sum_{j\ne i}J_{ij}\sigma_j.$$ One unit of this clock contains $n$ site updates on average. Write $P_t^J$ for this reversible semigroup. Its stationary spin autocorrelation and its critical rescaling are $$\begin{aligned}
 C_{n,J}(t)&=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
    \langle\sigma_i,P_t^J\sigma_i\rangle_{\pi_{n,J}},
       \label{eq:intro-correlation}\\
 A_{n,J}(s)&=n^{1/3}C_{n,J}(s n^{2/3}),\qquad s>0.
       \label{eq:intro-rescaling}
\end{aligned}$$ Thermal fluctuations and the randomness of the dynamics have already been averaged in these quantities. Their only remaining randomness is the interaction matrix.

Let $C_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,\infty))$ denote the continuous real functions, with uniform convergence on every compact subinterval of $(0,\infty)$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Functional universality). *There is a Borel probability law $Q_{\mathrm{crit}}$ on $C_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,\infty))$ such that, for each of the two choices of $D$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}_D(A_{n,J})\Longrightarrow Q_{\mathrm{crit}}
 \qquad(n\to\infty).$$ The law $Q_{\mathrm{crit}}$ is not a point mass. It assigns probability one to nonzero, nonnegative, nonincreasing functions $f$ satisfying $\lim_{s\to\infty}f(s)=0$. The Gaussian and Rademacher models use exactly the normalization in <a href="#eq:intro-rescaling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-rescaling">[eq:intro-rescaling]</a>.*

</div>

The restriction to positive times is necessary: $A_{n,J}(0)=n^{1/3}$. At every fixed positive time, the limiting value has unbounded support, as proved in Proposition <a href="#prop:functional-randomness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:functional-randomness">46</a>. The limiting law is described explicitly in Section <a href="#sec:edge-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:edge-law">2</a>. It is the law of a sum of coordinate autocorrelations for a reversible semigroup in countably many edge coordinates. The equilibrium measure of those coordinates is Gaussian conditioned on a renormalized quadratic constraint; its parameters are the random GOE edge points. A single positive deterministic coefficient accounts for the relaxation of microscopic spin gradients. Section <a href="#sec:microscopic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:microscopic">6</a> characterizes that coefficient by a determinate moment problem.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### History and relation to earlier work

Sherrington and Kirkpatrick introduced their infinite-range spin-glass model in 1975 (Sherrington and Kirkpatrick 1975) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[SK1975](#ref-SK1975)]. Dynamical mean-field theory subsequently studied relaxation through correlation and response, notably in the soft-spin framework of Sompolinsky and Zippelius (Sompolinsky and Zippelius 1982) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[SompolinskyZippelius1982](#ref-SompolinskyZippelius1982)]. For critical Ising heat-bath dynamics, Billoire and Campbell (Billoire and Campbell 2011, Equation (7)) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[BilloireCampbell2011](#ref-BilloireCampbell2011)] tested numerically the finite-size scaling form $$q_c(n,t)\sim n^{-1/3}F(t/n^{2/3}),$$ with time measured in updates per spin. This is the direct numerical precedent for <a href="#eq:intro-rescaling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-rescaling">[eq:intro-rescaling]</a>. A disorder-averaged scaling curve does not determine whether individual sample curves concentrate; our result identifies their limiting probability law.

Spherical models made the role of the spectral edge especially clear. Kosterlitz, Thouless, and Jones (Kosterlitz, Thouless, and Jones 1976) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[KosterlitzThoulessJones1976](#ref-KosterlitzThoulessJones1976)] identified the edge mechanism in the spherical spin-glass transition. Cugliandolo and Dean (Cugliandolo and Dean 1995) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[CugliandoloDean1995](#ref-CugliandoloDean1995)] analyzed Langevin relaxation and aging, and Ben Arous, Dembo, and Guionnet (Ben Arous, Dembo, and Guionnet 2001) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[BenArousDemboGuionnet2001](#ref-BenArousDemboGuionnet2001)] proved limiting dynamics and aging results for a soft spherical constraint. At finite size, Fyodorov, Perret, and Schehr (Fyodorov, Perret, and Schehr 2015) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[FyodorovPerretSchehr2015](#ref-FyodorovPerretSchehr2015)] related non-self-averaging on the $n^{2/3}$ time scale to edge fluctuations in zero-temperature spherical dynamics. These are spectral and dynamical predecessors, with different temperatures, initial conditions, and spin spaces from stationary critical Ising heat-bath dynamics.

The equilibrium comparison used here has its own history. Comets (Comets 1996) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Comets1996](#ref-Comets1996)] related Ising and spherical partition functions by averaging over the eigenframe. Baik and Lee (Baik and Lee 2016) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BaikLee2016](#ref-BaikLee2016)] studied spherical free-energy fluctuations on the two sides of the transition, and Landon (Landon 2022) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Landon2022](#ref-Landon2022)] analyzed critical free-energy fluctuations and the edge-scale saddle. Du and Huang (Du and Huang 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[DuHuang2026FreeEnergy](#ref-DuHuang2026FreeEnergy)] obtained a quantitative critical Ising–spherical partition comparison; their quenched overlap theorem (Du and Huang 2026b) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[DuHuang2026Overlap](#ref-DuHuang2026Overlap)] describes the random critical overlap law using Gaussian coordinates conditioned on a centered square sum. The conditioned measure used below follows this framework. The additional work here identifies the closed dynamical form, its microscopic coefficient, and the functional limit across two coupling laws.

Rigorous high-temperature mixing developed through spectral functional inequalities and localization. Relevant steps include Bauerschmidt and Bodineau (Bauerschmidt and Bodineau 2019) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[BauerschmidtBodineau2019](#ref-BauerschmidtBodineau2019)], Eldan, Koehler, and Zeitouni (Eldan, Koehler, and Zeitouni 2022) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[EldanKoehlerZeitouni2022](#ref-EldanKoehlerZeitouni2022)], and Anari, Koehler, and Vuong (Anari, Koehler, and Vuong 2024) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[AnariKoehlerVuong2024](#ref-AnariKoehlerVuong2024)]. For fixed inverse temperature $\beta<1/2$, Wang (Wang 2026) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Wang2026](#ref-Wang2026)] obtains $O_\beta(n\log(n/\epsilon))$ random-site heat-bath mixing, uniformly in external fields, with probability tending to one over the disorder. At zero field, Boban, Li, and Oveis Gharan (Boban, Li, and Oveis Gharan 2026) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[BobanLiOveisGharan2026](#ref-BobanLiOveisGharan2026)] obtain a spectral gap bounded below by a positive constant for rate-one-per-site dynamics, and polynomial mixing for $\beta<1/2+\epsilon_0$, with a universal $\epsilon_0>0$, again with high disorder probability. Such fixed-subcritical estimates do not identify dynamics at the endpoint $\beta=1$.

Two companion manuscripts supply the Gaussian estimates used below. The critical slowing-down manuscript (OpenAI 2026b, Theorems 1.1 and 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[CS](#ref-CS)] identifies the $n^{2/3}$ scale of covariance and linear relaxation, and gives an obstruction to mixing from most realized equilibrium starts at smaller times. The critical mixing manuscript (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[CM](#ref-CM)] obtains the matching mixing exponent and functional inequalities. Its comparison of spectral caps with spherical measures provides the starting estimates for our proof. An exponent statement alone does not identify the dynamics at a fixed multiple of $n^{2/3}$; we need quantitative improvements and an identification of the limiting energy. We state the companion inputs with their precise ranges in Section <a href="#sec:edge-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:edge-law">2</a> and prove those additional steps here.

Finally, the comparison of coupling laws belongs to the Lindeberg approach to universality (Chatterjee 2006) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Chatterjee2006](#ref-Chatterjee2006)]. Carmona and Hu (Carmona and Hu 2006) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[CarmonaHu2006](#ref-CarmonaHu2006)] proved universality of the SK limiting free energy. The observable here depends on the Gibbs law and the generator simultaneously. Its critical derivative bounds require the quadratic-feature comparison described next.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The proof and its main ingredients

We first work with Gaussian couplings. Adjoining an independent Gaussian diagonal does not affect the Gibbs law and makes the interaction matrix a GOE matrix $W=U\Lambda U^{\mathsf T}$. The spectral coordinates $x=n^{-1/3}U^{\mathsf T}\sigma$ isolate the fluctuations near the upper edge. Their limiting equilibrium law is a conditioned measure $\Pi$, rather than a product of independent Gaussian coordinates. This constraint remains visible in the domain of the limiting gradient form.

There are two separate reasons why equilibrium convergence does not already give the answer. First, a function of the edge coordinates can lower its Dirichlet energy by adding a small microscopic correction. Second, convergence of finitely many edge coordinates gives no bound on the sum over all coordinates in <a href="#eq:intro-correlation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-correlation">[eq:intro-correlation]</a>. The proof treats these issues before comparing the two coupling distributions.

The starting observation is the resolvent trace. If $H$ is minus the heat-bath generator, then for $z>0$ set $$B_n(z)=\sum_{a=1}^n
   \langle x_a,(z+n^{2/3}H)^{-1}x_a\rangle.$$ This quantity is independent of the choice of orthogonal coordinates. It is the Stieltjes transform of the same spectral measure whose Laplace transform is $A_{n,J}$. We establish convergence of these traces and then recover convergence of the functions on every compact positive time interval.

The observations used below have a concrete spectral meaning. At scale $p>0$, remove from $W$ the matrix $$B_p=U\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}\bigl((\lambda_a-(2-p^2))_+\bigr)U^{\mathsf T},
 \qquad h_p=B_p\sigma+\mathsf N(0,B_p).$$ Conditioning on $h_p$ replaces the interaction by $W-B_p$, whose eigenvalues are capped at $2-p^2$, and adds the external field $h_p$. Decreasing $p$ retains fewer observed directions near the spectral edge.

The Gaussian argument has four stages.

1.  Spectral observations smooth functions on the cube. We compare their posterior means with a spherical model and control the score of the comparison density. Sections <a href="#sec:observations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:observations">3</a> and <a href="#sec:variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:variance">4</a> sharpen the cap estimates to a conditional variance bound of order $p^{-2}$ at cap scale $p$, without a power loss. The gain comes from averaging the curvature error before compressing it onto a smaller random spectral subspace.

2.  In Section <a href="#sec:gradient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:gradient">5</a>, slopes of these smoothings converge to weak gradients for $\Pi$. We prove that their domain equals the closure of smooth cylinder functions. This identifies the closed energy form on the conditioned equilibrium measure.

3.  Section <a href="#sec:microscopic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:microscopic">6</a> identifies the energy remaining after microscopic correction. Fixed-order contractions specify a deterministic positive measure $\omega$ on $[0,\infty)$. Polynomial approximation isolates its atom $c_*=\omega(\{0\})$, producing matching lower bounds and recovery sequences for the limiting form $c_*\int|\nabla F|^2\,d\Pi$.

4.  Section <a href="#sec:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:trace">7</a> bounds the tail of the resolvent trace by vector integration by parts. The summable decay of the resulting coefficients makes finite-coordinate convergence sufficient for the whole observable.

For Rademacher couplings we compare basis-independent traces directly. The Lindeberg argument in Section <a href="#sec:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:comparison">8</a> first transfers a trace built from quadratic spin features. Its vanishing controls the fourth-order remainders for the spin trace. This order of comparison is what allows Gaussian and sign couplings, whose fourth moments differ, to have the same limit. Finally, Section <a href="#sec:functional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:functional">9</a> proves the functional convergence, decay, and surviving disorder randomness. Appendix <a href="#sec:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:support">10</a> explains why almost-sure decay is compatible with nondecaying functions in the closed topological support.

<a id="sec:edge-law"></a>
### The conditioned edge measure and the Gaussian inputs

The limiting state space is determined by the upper spectral edge of the interaction matrix. The spherical model makes this state space explicit: after diagonalization, its only remaining interaction is a constraint on the sum of the squared coordinates. We construct the resulting probability measure on infinitely many coordinates, following the equilibrium edge-conditioning description of Du–Huang (Du and Huang 2026b, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[DuHuang2026Overlap](#ref-DuHuang2026Overlap)]. We then explain the change of disorder law that transfers spherical calculations to the cube, and record the cap estimates used in the dynamical argument.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The heat-bath form and the edge coordinates

<a id="eq:edge-heatbath-form"></a>
Write $\mu=\pi_{n,J}$ and put $T=n^{2/3}$. For a function on the cube, let $$D_i f(X)=\frac{f(X^{i,+})-f(X^{i,-})}{2},\qquad
 t_i(X)=\tanh h_i(X),\qquad w_i(X)=1-t_i(X)^2,$$ where $X^{i,\pm}$ is obtained by setting the $i$th spin to $\pm1$ and $h_i(X)=n^{-1/2}\sum_{j\ne i}J_{ij}X_j$. The nonnegative heat-bath operator and its Dirichlet form are $$\label{eq:edge-heatbath-form}
 H=\sum_{i=1}^n(X_i-t_i)D_i,
 \qquad
 \mathcal E(f,g)=\mu\!\left[\sum_{i=1}^n w_iD_ifD_ig\right],
 \qquad \mathcal E(f)=\mathcal E(f,f).$$ Thus $P_t^J=e^{-tH}$. Forms and inner products of vector-valued functions include summation over the vector index. In particular, the clock normalization in <a href="#eq:edge-heatbath-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:edge-heatbath-form">[eq:edge-heatbath-form]</a> is rate one at each site.

Until Section <a href="#sec:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:comparison">8</a>, the couplings are Gaussian. Let $W$ have off-diagonal entries $J_{ij}/\sqrt n$, and add independent diagonal entries with law $N(0,2/n)$. The extra energy is constant on the cube, so it changes neither $\mu$ nor $H$. The completed matrix is GOE. Diagonalize it as $$W=U\Lambda U^{\mathsf T},\qquad
 \lambda_1\ge\cdots\ge\lambda_n,
 \qquad d_a=2-\lambda_a,\qquad g_{a,n}=n^{2/3}d_a.$$ After independent choices of eigenvector signs, $U$ is Haar orthogonal conditionally on $\Lambda$. Its columns are denoted by $v_a$. Set $$Y_a=v_a^{\mathsf T}X,\qquad x_a=n^{-1/3}Y_a,
 \qquad
 B_n(z)=\sum_{a=1}^n\langle x_a,(z+TH)^{-1}x_a\rangle_\mu,
 \quad z>0.$$ The trace $B_n(z)$ is independent of the chosen orthonormal basis and is the Laplace transform of the rescaled autocorrelation: $$B_n(z)=\int_0^\infty e^{-zs}A_{n,J}(s)\,ds.$$ We use $x_a$ also for the coordinate functions of the limiting measure.

Let $\rho_{\rm sc}$ be the semicircle probability measure $$d\rho_{\rm sc}(\lambda)=\frac1{2\pi}
 \sqrt{4-\lambda^2}\,\mathbf 1_{[-2,2]}(\lambda)\,d\lambda.$$ The GOE edge convergence theorem gives the joint convergence of each fixed number of $g_{a,n}$ to a simple, increasing point process $g=(g_1,g_2,\ldots)$; see (Ramírez, Rider, and Virág 2011) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[RamirezRiderVirag2011](#ref-RamirezRiderVirag2011)]. This definition fixes the sign and scale of the edge process throughout the paper.

<a id="stmt-lem-edge-spectral"></a>
<div id="lem:edge-spectral" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Spectral bounds). *The GOE eigenvalues have the following additional properties.*

1.  *$\max_a|\lambda_a|\le3$ with probability tending to one.*

2.  *There are tight random bounds $K_n\ge0$ and $C_n>0$ such that $g_{a,n}\ge C_n^{-1}a^{2/3}$ for every $a>K_n$.*

<a id="eq:edge-trace-double-limit"></a>
3.  *For every $\epsilon>0$, $$\label{eq:edge-trace-double-limit}
     \lim_{L\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}
     \mathbb P\!\left(
     g_{1,n}+L\le0\ \text{or}\
     \left|n^{1/3}-\sum_{a=1}^n\frac1{g_{a,n}+L}-\sqrt L\right|
           >\epsilon\right)=0.$$*

*In the event appearing in the last formula, the sum is evaluated only when $g_{1,n}+L>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-edge-spectral"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-edge-spectral</summary>

*Proof.* We use the eigenvalue-only spectral estimate of (OpenAI 2026b, Proposition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[CS](#ref-CS)]. For every deterministic sequence $L_n\to\infty$ with $L_n\le\log n$, that proposition controls both resolvent traces for $s\in[L_nn^{-2/3},5]$, with positive denominators. At $s=L_nn^{-2/3}$, multiplying its first-trace error by $n^{1/3}$ gives $$O\!\left(L_n^{-1}+L_n^{-1/8}
       +L_n^{-1/4}\sqrt{\log(2+L_n)}\right)=o(1).$$ The semicircle resolvent equals $(2+s-\sqrt{s(4+s)})/2$, whose expansion supplies the main term $\sqrt{L_n}$ and an additional error $O(L_nn^{-1/3})=o(1)$.

The second-trace estimate yields, for the same interval of $u$, $$\#\{a:d_a\le u\}
 \le 4u^2\sum_a(d_a+u)^{-2}
 \le Cnu^{3/2}.$$ Indeed $0<d_a+u\le2u$ for every counted eigenvalue. It follows that $g_{a,n}\ge c a^{2/3}$ once $a>CL_n^{3/2}$. The uniform norm bound is part of the same proposition.

These conclusions hold for every arbitrarily slowly divergent $L_n$. If <a href="#eq:edge-trace-double-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:edge-trace-double-limit">[eq:edge-trace-double-limit]</a> failed, one could select $L_j\to\infty$ and $n_j\to\infty$, with $\log n_j\ge L_j$, along which the failure probability stayed positive, and embed the values $L_{n_j}=L_j$ in a sufficiently slow divergent sequence. This contradicts the preceding estimate. The identical criterion, applied to the least index after which the counting consequence holds, makes that index tight. This proves the second assertion as well. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-edge-tight-convergence"></a>
<div id="rem:edge-tight-convergence" class="remark">

*Remark 3* (Convergence on sets of large disorder probability). We will use constants that are bounded on disorder events of probability arbitrarily close to one. A bound of this kind is called a tight bound. For subsequential arguments, include these bounds, the eigenvalues, and the countably many relevant error quantities in the joint law before taking a Skorokhod representation. On the represented space, finite bounds and coordinate convergence can then be used deterministically. The conditional laws of the remaining finite-dimensional variables lift this representation to the original finite problems. A further diagonal extraction handles quantities that vanish in an iterated limit such as <a href="#eq:edge-trace-double-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:edge-trace-double-limit">[eq:edge-trace-double-limit]</a>. The representation itself makes no assertion of convergence for the original sequence: one must identify every subsequential limit. Once this identification is made, the subsequence criterion gives the asserted joint convergence in distribution. In particular, if every subsequence of an intrinsic error quantity has a further represented subsequence tending to zero, that quantity tends to zero in probability on the original disorder space. No uniform integrability on exceptional disorders is required.

A separate uniformity convention is essential for variational arguments. When an estimate is applied to a disorder-dependent test, its quenched inequality is understood to hold on one event for every test in the stated class, with the displayed bounds on its norm, energy, and polynomial growth. One way to obtain such an event, used below, is to bound the error for every test by its stated norms times a common nonnegative random error $R_n$. Convergence $R_n\to0$ then permits arbitrary measurable choices of the test. Convergence on a countable core alone is not used to infer this uniformity.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Conditioning the infinite Gaussian family

Choose an admissible parameter $b$ with $b+g_1>0$, and write $l_a=(g_a+b)^{-1}$. A convenient measurable choice is $b=1+(-g_1)_+$; use the corresponding $b_n=1+(-g_{1,n})_+$ in finite dimension. By Lemma <a href="#lem:edge-spectral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:edge-spectral">2</a>, $$l_{a,n}=(g_{a,n}+b_n)^{-1}\longrightarrow l_a,
 \qquad
 \lim_{m\to\infty}\sup_n\sum_{a>m}l_{a,n}^2=0$$ on the represented sets just described, after discarding finitely many initial $n$. In particular $\sum_a l_a^2<\infty$.

<a id="eq:source-1"></a>
For independent $Z_a\sim N(0,l_a)$, the centered series $S=\sum_a(Z_a^2-l_a)$ converges almost surely and in $L^2$. Define $$\label{eq:source-1}
 D(b)=\lim_{L\to\infty}\left\{
 \sqrt L-\sum_{a\ge1}\left(\frac1{g_a+b}-\frac1{g_a+L}\right)\right\}.$$ Here $L$ is restricted to values with $L+g_1>0$. The limit is in probability with respect to $g$; a deterministic subsequence gives an almost sure version. The next proposition both justifies the limit and gives a pointwise meaning to conditioning on $S=D(b)$.

<a id="stmt-prop-edge-conditioned-law"></a>
<div id="prop:edge-conditioned-law" class="proposition">

**Proposition 4** (The conditioned edge measure). *Formula <a href="#eq:source-1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-1">[eq:source-1]</a> defines a finite random variable, and $$D_n(b_n):=n^{1/3}-\sum_{a=1}^n l_{a,n}
       \ \Longrightarrow\ D(b)$$ jointly with the edge coordinates. Almost surely $\sum_a l_a=\infty$. The law of $(Z_a)_{a\ge1}$ conditioned on $S=D(b)$ has a version $\Pi=\Pi_g$ characterized by continuous conditional densities. It is independent of the admissible choice of $b$.*

<a id="eq:edge-coordinate-moments"></a>
*Let $\mu^{\rm sp}$ denote normalized surface measure on the sphere of radius $\sqrt n$, tilted by $\exp(X^{\mathsf T}WX/2)$. The fixed-coordinate marginals of $n^{-1/3}U^{\mathsf T}X$ under $\mu^{\rm sp}$ converge jointly in distribution with the eigenvalues to the random marginals of $\Pi$. On the spectral representations of Remark <a href="#rem:edge-tight-convergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rem:edge-tight-convergence">3</a>, this is ordinary weak convergence of measures, including convergence of every fixed polynomial moment. On the sets in Remark <a href="#rem:edge-tight-convergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rem:edge-tight-convergence">3</a>, for every fixed $0\le j<\infty$ one has $$\label{eq:edge-coordinate-moments}
 \int |x_a|^j\,d\Pi\le C_j l_a^{j/2},
 \qquad
 \mu^{\rm sp}|x_a|^j\le C_j l_{a,n}^{j/2},$$ uniformly in the retained coordinate $a$.*

<a id="eq:edge-polar-radius"></a>
*For any sufficiently large fixed $m$, the tail $(x_a)_{a>m}$ under $\Pi$ has a density relative to $\bigotimes_{a>m}N(0,l_a)$. Conditional on this tail, the first $m$ coordinates lie on the sphere of squared radius $$\label{eq:edge-polar-radius}
 D(b)+\sum_{a\le m}l_a-\sum_{a>m}(x_a^2-l_a),$$ with the Gaussian angular density on that sphere. In particular, $\sum_a(x_a^2-l_a)=D(b)$ holds $\Pi$-almost surely.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-edge-conditioned-law"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-edge-conditioned-law</summary>

*Proof.* For fixed admissible $L$, the identity $$D_n(b_n)=n^{1/3}-\sum_a\frac1{g_{a,n}+L}
 -\sum_a\left(\frac1{g_{a,n}+b_n}-\frac1{g_{a,n}+L}\right)$$ separates the divergent trace from an absolutely convergent difference. The tail of the second sum is bounded by a constant times $|L-b_n|\sum_{a>m}a^{-4/3}$ on each set of bounded spectral data, so that sum passes to the limit. Lemma <a href="#lem:edge-spectral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:edge-spectral">2</a> then shows that the expressions in braces in <a href="#eq:source-1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-1">[eq:source-1]</a> are Cauchy in probability, and that $D_n(b_n)$ has the asserted joint limit. If $\sum_a l_a$ were finite, those expressions would tend to $+\infty$ as $L\to\infty$; this contradicts the finite limit. Changing $b$ changes $D(b)$ by the corresponding absolutely convergent resolvent difference.

We give the density argument explicitly. Let $q$ be the density of $S$, and let $q_m$ be the density of $\sum_{a>m}(Z_a^2-l_a)$. After any fixed coordinates are omitted, a sufficiently large fixed group of remaining coordinates supplies an integrable bound for the characteristic function: the modulus of the factor for coordinate $a$ is $(1+4t^2l_a^2)^{-1/4}$. Fourier inversion therefore gives continuous, bounded densities. The same bound applies to the finite sums and proves uniform convergence of their densities when the variances converge and their squared tails vanish.

<a id="eq:edge-cylinder-density"></a>
The centered sum after any finite deletion has support all of $\mathbb R$. Its unbounded right support comes from one square. To reach an arbitrarily negative interval, make a sufficiently long finite block of squares small, using $\sum l_a=\infty$, and keep the remaining centered sum in a bounded interval of positive probability. Convolution with a finite block whose square-sum density is positive in the interior of its support now gives $q(s)>0$ and $q_m(s)>0$ for every $s\in\mathbb R$. Thus the first $m$ coordinates of the required conditional law have density $$\label{eq:edge-cylinder-density}
 \frac{q_m\!\left(D(b)-\sum_{a\le m}(x_a^2-l_a)\right)}{q(D(b))}
 \prod_{a\le m}\frac{e^{-x_a^2/(2l_a)}}{\sqrt{2\pi l_a}}.$$ Convolution shows that these densities are consistent. They depend measurably on the spectral data, so their extension is a random probability measure $\Pi_g$.

For completeness, they admit a disintegration on the constraint itself. Let $q^{\rm head}_m$ be the density of $\sum_{a\le m}(Z_a^2-l_a)$. Give the tail product Gaussian law the weight $$\frac{q^{\rm head}_m\!\left(D(b)-\sum_{a>m}(x_a^2-l_a)\right)}{q(D(b))}.$$ Given the tail, use polar disintegration of the first $m$ Gaussian coordinates at the radius <a href="#eq:edge-polar-radius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:edge-polar-radius">[eq:edge-polar-radius]</a>. This produces <a href="#eq:edge-cylinder-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:edge-cylinder-density">[eq:edge-cylinder-density]</a>, has an absolutely continuous tail law, and enforces the constraint almost surely.

In finite dimension, conditioning independent normals of variances $l_{a,n}$ on $\sum_a x_a^2=n^{1/3}$ gives exactly the spherical Gibbs measure in the stated coordinates: the term involving $b_n$ in the Gaussian exponent is constant on that sphere. The density convergence above and $q(D(b))>0$ imply convergence of each cylinder marginal. Their densities are bounded by a constant times the corresponding product Gaussian density. To retain an arbitrary single coordinate uniformly, choose the integrable Fourier bound from a fixed group of leading coordinates other than that coordinate. This proves <a href="#eq:edge-coordinate-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:edge-coordinate-moments">[eq:edge-coordinate-moments]</a> and uniform integrability of all fixed polynomial moments.

The argument applies to every admissible $b$ on sets where its distance from the pole is bounded below and its magnitude is bounded above; these sets exhaust the admissible choices. The finite conditioned spherical law is independent of $b_n$, so its limiting cylinder marginals are also independent of $b$. The conclusion holds simultaneously for all admissible $b$. First take a common probability-one event for rational parameters. On every compact admissible interval, the resolvent-difference series converges uniformly, so $D(b)$ is continuous. The squared variance tails and the Fourier majorants above are also uniform on that interval. Thus the conditional cylinder densities in <a href="#eq:edge-cylinder-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:edge-cylinder-density">[eq:edge-cylinder-density]</a>, and their integrals against bounded continuous cylinder tests, depend continuously on $b$. Equality for rational parameters therefore extends to the whole interval. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-edge-bulk-weight"></a>
<div id="lem:edge-bulk-weight" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (The bulk spectral weight). *For a spherical Gibbs spin, the random probability measure $$\frac1n\sum_a Y_a^2\delta_{\lambda_a}$$ converges in probability, conditionally on the spectral data as above, to $(2-\lambda)^{-1}d\rho_{\rm sc}(\lambda)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-edge-bulk-weight"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-edge-bulk-weight</summary>

*Proof.* First test against a bounded continuous function supported away from $2$. Before conditioning, the Gaussian variance of its integral is $O(n^{-1})$, and its mean converges to the stated integral by the semicircle law. Integrate out a fixed number of leading Gaussian coordinates when imposing the norm constraint. Their square-sum density gives a bounded density ratio, so the concentration persists after conditioning. Each of those leading coordinates has mass $n^{-1/3}x_a^2=o(1)$ in probability by <a href="#eq:edge-coordinate-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:edge-coordinate-moments">[eq:edge-coordinate-moments]</a>. Finally both the finite measure and its proposed limit have mass one, since $\int(2-\lambda)^{-1}d\rho_{\rm sc}(\lambda)=1$. This extends convergence across the edge. The same argument permits bounded tests with cuts at continuity points in the bulk. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The limiting autocorrelation and its time constant

<a id="eq:source-2"></a>
For a smooth compactly supported cylinder function $F$ on $\mathbb R^{\mathbb N}$, consider the quadratic form $$\mathcal E_\Pi(F)=\int\sum_a|\partial_aF|^2\,d\Pi.$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:gradient-domain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gradient-domain">22</a> will prove that this form is closable and identify its domain. Denote the nonnegative operator associated to its closure by $H_\Pi$. The law in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is specified by $$\label{eq:source-2}
 \mathcal A(s)=\sum_{a\ge1}
 \langle x_a,e^{-s c_*H_\Pi}x_a\rangle_\Pi,
 \qquad s>0,$$ where $c_*>0$ is deterministic. Finiteness, continuity, and the remaining assertions about this random function are proved in Sections <a href="#sec:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:trace">7</a> and <a href="#sec:functional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:functional">9</a>.

The coefficient is $c_*=\omega(\{0\})$, where $\omega$ is the deterministic finite positive measure of microscopic heat-bath energies characterized by the moment prescription <a href="#eq:source-3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-3">[eq:source-3]</a> in Section <a href="#sec:microscopic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:microscopic">6</a>. Proposition <a href="#prop:micro-moment-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:micro-moment-measure">26</a> constructs this measure and proves its determinacy; Theorem <a href="#thm:micro-relaxation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:micro-relaxation">33</a> proves that its atom at zero is strictly positive and is the coefficient in <a href="#eq:source-2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-2">[eq:source-2]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Haar bias and Gaussian cap observations

We now connect the spherical calculations with the cube. All partition functions use probability reference measures: uniform probability on the cube and normalized surface measure on the sphere. Conditional on the spectrum, put $$L_\circ(U)=\frac{Z_{\rm cube}(W)}{Z_{\rm sp}(W)}.$$ Haar averaging gives $E_U L_\circ=1$. Define the probability law $\mathbf P$, and its expectation $\mathbf E$, by drawing $U$ with Haar density $L_\circ$, then drawing a cube spin with law $\mu$. Any observation noises introduced below are independent of this spin conditionally on the matrix. The spectrum is held fixed in $\mathbf P$ and $\mathbf E$.

<a id="stmt-lem-edge-biased-law"></a>
<div id="lem:edge-biased-law" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:edge-uncapped-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Spherical identity under Haar bias). *Under $\mathbf P$, $U^{\mathsf T}X$ has exactly the spherical Gibbs law in spectral coordinates. After the row sign change sending $X$ to $\mathbf1$, conditional on $y=U^{\mathsf T}X$ the eigenframe is Haar subject to $Uy=\mathbf1$. Moreover, on the eigenvalue events used below, $$\label{eq:edge-uncapped-comparison}
 E_U(L_\circ-1)^2=o(1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-edge-biased-law"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-edge-biased-law</summary>

*Proof.* In the joint weighted law, $Z_{\rm cube}$ cancels the Gibbs denominator. For each fixed cube vertex, Haar rotation sends that vertex to uniform surface measure on the sphere of radius $\sqrt n$. The remaining energy weight is $\exp(y^{\mathsf T}\Lambda y/2)$, proving the first assertion. The same invariance gives the conditional Haar law on the stated coset. Finally, the uncapped zero-field calculation in the proof of the static cap comparison of (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[CM](#ref-CM)], Section 15, paragraph “Zero field and overlaps bounded away from zero,” gives $E_UZ_{\rm cube}^2/(Z_{\rm sp})^2=1+o(1)$. Together with $E_UL_\circ=1$, this is <a href="#eq:edge-uncapped-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:edge-uncapped-comparison">[eq:edge-uncapped-comparison]</a>. ◻

</details>

For example, if $G\ge0$ is a spin or observation functional, then $$E_U\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\{L_\circ\ge1/2\}}Q_\mu G\right]
       \le2\mathbf E G.$$ Here $Q_\mu$ denotes the ordinary joint law of the Gibbs spin and its observations at fixed disorder. This inequality and Markov’s inequality transfer biased mean estimates to quenched estimates; the discarded disorder event has probability $o(1)$ by <a href="#eq:edge-uncapped-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:edge-uncapped-comparison">[eq:edge-uncapped-comparison]</a>.

The cap at scale $p>0$ is defined by $$P_p=\mathbf 1_{\{d\le p^2\}},\quad \mathcal H_p=\operatorname{ran}P_p,\quad
 B_p=(p^2-d)_+,\quad W_p=W-B_p,\quad k_p=np^3,\quad M_p=\sqrt{np}.$$ Matrix functions in these formulas are conjugated by $U$ when used in physical coordinates. Figure <a href="#fig:spectral-cap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:spectral-cap">1</a> shows how the cap flattens the upper edge and how the removed part becomes observation precision.

<figure id="fig:spectral-cap">

<figcaption>A spectral cap, shown near the upper edge. The posterior interaction $W_{p}$ retains eigenvalues below $2 − p^{2}$ and flattens those above it. The removed amount $(λ−(2−p^{2}))_{+}$ is the precision of the Gaussian observation in that eigendirection. The drawing is schematic; its geometry illustrates the defining spectral functions, not an eigenvalue density.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-spectral-cap"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:spectral-cap`)

 \draw[gray,dashed,thick] (0.35,0.35)--(3.85,3.85);

   {observation\\precision};

The horizontal axis has marked values $2 - p^2$ and $2$. A diagonal line labeled "original eigenvalue" starts from the origin and continues upward with slope 1, becoming dashed for $\lambda > 2 - p^2$. A solid blue path labeled "$\min(\lambda, 2 - p^2)$" follows this diagonal up to the point $(2 - p^2, 2 - p^2)$, then flattens into a horizontal line at height $2 - p^2$. Vertical dashed lines connect $(2 - p^2, 2 - p^2)$ to $2 - p^2$ on the $\lambda$-axis and the dashed diagonal at $\lambda = 2$ to $2$ on the $\lambda$-axis. The region above the horizontal blue line and below the dashed diagonal line is shaded light purple. A vertical double-headed arrow spanning this shaded vertical gap is labeled "observation precision". Observe $h_p=B_pX+\mathsf N(0,B_p)$, with independent Gaussian increments as $B_p$ increases, starting from zero precision if necessary. Bayes’ formula makes the posterior interaction $W_p$ and the posterior field $h_p$. Write $\mu_{p,h}$ for the cube posterior at a specified field $h$. Let $\phi_p(h)$ be its log partition function, $m_p(h)=\nabla\phi_p(h)$ its mean, and $K_p(h)=P_p\nabla^2\phi_p(h)P_p$ its covariance restricted to $\mathcal H_p$. Superscript ${\rm sp}$ denotes the corresponding spherical quantities. At path fields we often suppress the argument $h_p$.

<a id="eq:source-4"></a>
On the joint space of the eigenframe and the spectral observation path, the density of $\mathbf P$ relative to independent Haar measure and the spherical observation law, through scale $p$, is $$\label{eq:source-4}
 L_p=\exp\{\phi_p(h_p)-\phi_p^{\rm sp}(h_p)\}.$$ Indeed the likelihood of the observations is a common Gaussian factor multiplied by the posterior partition function divided by the original one; the factor $L_\circ$ cancels the two original partition functions. The spin in spectral coordinates already has the spherical law by Lemma <a href="#lem:edge-biased-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:edge-biased-law">6</a>; its observation noises are independent Gaussian increments with covariance determined by the spectrum. Hence the entire spectral observation path under $\mathbf P$ has exactly its spherical law. We normalize its coordinates by $$u_{p,a}=\frac{(U^{\mathsf T}h_p)_a}{p^2n^{1/3}},
 \qquad r=n^{-1/3+\delta},\qquad \delta=10^{-4}.$$ We will use $r\le p\le p_{\rm top}$, allowing fixed-factor buffers at the endpoints. The values $d_a=p^2$ cause no discontinuity in the observations; they may be omitted in scale integrals, and zero-variance coordinates are omitted from density formulas.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Saddles and cap estimates

For a field $h\in\mathcal H_p$, its spherical saddle $\alpha=\alpha_p(h)$ is the unique solution above the pole of <a id="eq:source-5"></a>$$\label{eq:source-5}
 R_{p,\alpha}=(\max(d,p^2)+\alpha)^{-1},\qquad
 n=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}R_{p,\alpha}+\|R_{p,\alpha}h\|^2.$$ The annulus $|\alpha_p(h)|\le s_0p^2$ is separated from zero field. Its radius as a function of $\alpha$ is $$\ell_p(\alpha)=(p^2+\alpha)\sqrt{n-\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}R_{p,\alpha}}.$$ The following statement collects the precise Gaussian inputs needed later. All upper cutoffs and tolerances in it are fixed constants before $n$ tends to infinity.

<a id="stmt-prop-edge-cap-inputs"></a>
<div id="prop:edge-cap-inputs" class="proposition">

**Proposition 7** (Cap inputs). *Fix a finite field radius $R$ and positive fixed error tolerances. There are sufficiently small constants $p_{\rm top},s_0>0$ such that the following hold with probability tending to one in the range $np^3\ge c\sqrt{\log n}$ and $p\le p_{\rm top}$.*

**Spectral and spherical estimates, at every scale.* The spectral dimension satisfies $\dim\mathcal H_p\asymp k_p$, $d_1\ge-o(p^2)$ uniformly in the cap range, and $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}R_{p,0}^2=O(n/p)$. On $|\alpha|\le s_0p^2$, $\ell_p(\alpha)\asymp p^2M_p$ and $\ell_p'(\alpha)\asymp M_p$. For $\|h\|\le Rp^2M_p$, the saddle precision has gap comparable to $p^2$. At $\alpha=\alpha_p(h)$, the density at $n$ of the squared norm of $\mathsf N(R_{p,\alpha}h,R_{p,\alpha})$ is comparable to $\sqrt{p/n}$. After multiplication by $e^{v^{\mathsf T}X}$ and renormalization, the Gaussian mean becomes $R_{p,\alpha}(h+v)$ while its covariance remains $R_{p,\alpha}$; its squared-norm density at $n$ is at most a fixed multiple of $\sqrt{p/n}$. For $m$ replicas, where $m$ is fixed, consider rowwise linear or quadratic tilts that preserve Gaussianity. The joint density of their squared norms is at most $C_m(p/n)^{m/2}$ under the following hypotheses. The spectral rows remain independent after the rowwise tilt, the tilted precision is positive definite, and on at least $ck_p$ rows the post-tilt row covariance $C_a$ satisfies $c p^{-2}\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}_m\preceq C_a\preceq C p^{-2}\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}_m$. All comparison constants here are fixed.*

<a id="eq:source-6"></a>
**Curvature estimates, at every scale.* On the annulus, for any prescribed fixed $\epsilon_0>0$, $$\label{eq:source-6}
 K_p\preceq(1+\epsilon_0)p^{-2}\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathcal H_p},\qquad
 p^2(K_p)_{ee}\le1-\epsilon_1,
 \quad e=h/\|h\|,\quad \epsilon_1=0.1.$$ On the entire field ball one also has $K_p\preceq C_Rp^{-2}\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathcal H_p}$, where $C_R$ may be chosen before $\epsilon_0$.*

**Static comparisons and cube projection tails, on geometric lists.* For any prescribed fixed geometric list of scales, uniformly over the field ball, $$|\phi_p(h)-\phi_p^{\rm sp}(h)|\le\epsilon_v k_p,
 \qquad
 \|P_pm_p(h)-R_{p,\alpha_p(h)}h\|\le\epsilon_gM_p.$$ Any fixed finite family of such lists is allowed. For each $h\in\mathcal H_p$ with $\|h\|\le Rp^2M_p$, the cube posterior $\mu_{p,h}$ satisfies, uniformly for every $v\ge v_0$ with a sufficiently large fixed threshold $v_0$, $$\mu_{p,h}\{\|P_pX\|>vM_p\}\le C\exp(-ak_pv^6).$$ For $v>p^{-1/2}$ the event is empty, since $\|X\|=\sqrt n$. Here $a>0$, and the same bound holds under the uncapped zero-field cube measure $\mu$ at the same list scales.*

*The spherical projection-tail estimate holds at every scale in the stated range, for capped bounded-field and uncapped zero-field measures. Also, at every such cap scale, for independent spherical replicas at a common field in the prescribed ball, $$(\mu_{p,h}^{\rm sp})^{\otimes2}
 \{|n^{-1}X^{\mathsf T}X'|>\rho\}
 \le C\exp(-cn\rho^3),\qquad Cp\le\rho\le\rho_0,$$ for a sufficiently small fixed $\rho_0>0$. A fixed finite number of replicas obeys the corresponding bound for any pair.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-edge-cap-inputs"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-edge-cap-inputs</summary>

*Source statements and the radial consequence.* The spectral, saddle, and density assertions are the conclusions of (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[CM](#ref-CM)], Sections 15.2–15.3, “Spectral estimates at the bottom scale” and “Spherical saddles, norm densities, and tails.” The static comparison and its projection tails are Proposition 15.1 in that section. Its order of choices is the one stated here: fix the radius, errors, and geometric ratios, then decrease the upper cutoff. The overlap estimate is the spherical estimate preceding that proposition.

The upper covariance bounds are Theorem 18.6, “Uniform curvature of the true cap potentials,” in (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[CM](#ref-CM)]. Its lower endpoint is $(\sqrt{\log n}/n)^{1/3}$, with fixed-factor changes permitted, so it covers our scale $r$ and its fixed buffers. We also use a consequence retained in that theorem’s proof. Let $\gamma$ be the fixed geometric ratio chosen in that proof, and set $R_{p,\alpha}^H=(P_pR_{p,\alpha}P_p)|_{\mathcal H_p}$. At an intermediate evaluation ratio $x\in[\gamma^2,\gamma]$, Lemma 16.2, “Evaluation with the induction extension,” in (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[CM](#ref-CM)] bounds the radial entry of $p^2(K_p-R_{p,\alpha}^H)$ above by $-0.26$. The theorem’s proof chooses $s_0$ so that every target saddle lies inside the parent agreement annulus with fixed slack, and applies exactly this deterministic evaluation. Therefore $$p^2(K_p)_{ee}\le\frac1{1+\alpha/p^2}-0.26
                  \le\frac1{1-s_0}-0.26\le0.9$$ after decreasing $s_0$. This proves the second part of <a href="#eq:source-6" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-6">[eq:source-6]</a> with a fixed tolerance. ◻

</details>

The list restriction in the static comparison is harmless, but its removal requires a short argument because later observation identities integrate over all scales.

<a id="stmt-lem-edge-static-interpolation"></a>
<div id="lem:edge-static-interpolation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Interpolation between cap scales). *The value, projected-mean, and cube projection-tail conclusions of Proposition <a href="#prop:edge-cap-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:edge-cap-inputs">7</a> hold at every scale in the stated range, after prescribing slightly smaller errors on a sufficiently fine fixed geometric list and decreasing the upper cutoff.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-edge-static-interpolation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-edge-static-interpolation</summary>

*Proof.* First use an ordinary fixed-ratio list. Monotonicity of $P_p$ and a fixed-factor change in $M_p$ extend the uncapped projection tail to every $p$. Removing $B_p$ from the interaction changes its normalizing factor by $$\mu\exp(-X^{\mathsf T}B_pX/2)\ge
 \mu\{\|P_pX\|\le VM_p\}\,
       \exp(-\|B_p\|V^2M_p^2/2)\ge e^{-Ck_p},$$ where $V$ is fixed and large. We used $\|B_p\|=O(p^2)$ on the spectral event. Thus the projection tail persists at cap zero field. A field $\|h\|\le Rp^2M_p$ adds at most $Rk_pv$ on the shell $\|P_pX\|=vM_p$; this is dominated by $ak_pv^6$ for large $v$. The zero-field measure is even, so its linear-tilt normalizer is at least one. This gives the same tail on the field ball, with an adjusted starting threshold. The argument also bounds $\|P_qX\|/M_q$ under the $p$-cap posterior whenever $q\ge p$ and $q/p$ is bounded: use the uncapped $P_q$ tail, the same denominator lower bound, and $P_ph=h\in\mathcal H_q$. This is the tail needed to compare two nearby caps. The spherical estimates give the identical conclusion.

Now choose a fixed geometric list with consecutive ratios close to one, and let $q$ be its first scale above $p$. Then $\mathcal H_p\subset\mathcal H_q$ and $$\|W_p-W_q\|\le C(1-p/q)q^2,
 \qquad \operatorname{ran}(W_p-W_q)\subset\mathcal H_q.$$ For $h\in\mathcal H_p$ in the prescribed ball, restrict first to $\|P_qX\|\le VM_q$. The change in log weight there is at most $C(1-p/q)V^2k_q$. On its complement, the projection tail controls the exponential of the quadratic change: the exponent $C(1-p/q)k_qv^2$ is dominated by the negative $ak_qv^6$ tail. Choose $V$ first and the list ratio second. The resulting log-partition change is as small as desired in units $k_q$, for both cube and sphere. Their value comparison at $q$ therefore implies the value comparison at $p$, since $k_p/k_q$ is arbitrarily close to one.

For the mean, use the value comparison on a slightly larger field ball. The frozen Gaussian saddle identity and its norm-density bound give, for any unit $v\in\mathcal H_p$ and both signs, $$\log\mu_{p,h}e^{\pm t v^{\mathsf T}(X-R_{p,\alpha}h)}
       \le 2\epsilon_v k_p+\tfrac12t^2v^{\mathsf T}R_{p,\alpha}v+C.$$ Jensen’s inequality, with $t=\theta p^2M_p$, bounds the projected mean discrepancy divided by $M_p$ by $C\theta+(2\epsilon_v+C/k_p)/\theta$. Choose $\theta$ from the required mean tolerance and then $\epsilon_v$ sufficiently small. This proves the claim uniformly at every intermediate scale. ◻

</details>

The estimates of this section supply the ordinary Gaussian caps and the spherical description of their observations. The next section derives the quantitative comparison and score estimates needed to retain the exact $n^{2/3}$ time scale.

<a id="sec:observations"></a>
### Quantitative estimates for the observations

The coarse cap estimates of Proposition <a href="#prop:edge-cap-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:edge-cap-inputs">7</a> locate the relevant fields and bound their covariance by a constant times $p^{-2}$. For the time normalization in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, we shall need a relative covariance error that is integrable in logarithmic scale. The purpose of this section is to obtain the averaged estimates from which that improvement will follow. There are two different sources of smallness: a two-replica comparison at the smallest observation scale, and an integrated likelihood estimate at the larger scales.

We retain the notation and conditional-on-spectrum probability $\mathbf P$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:edge-biased-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:edge-biased-law">6</a>. Thus $\mathbf P$ includes the partition-function bias on the eigenframe and the ordinary Gibbs observation path given that frame; $Q_\mu$ denotes the latter path law for fixed disorder. Expectations with respect to these laws are denoted by $\mathbf E$ and $Q_\mu[\,\cdot\,]$. Constants in the present section are uniform on each of the tight spectral sets described in Remark <a href="#rem:edge-tight-convergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rem:edge-tight-convergence">3</a>. They may depend on that set, on a fixed moment order, and on fixed field or scale ratios. A bound $C\exp(-n^\xi)$ always has some $\xi>0$; decreasing $\xi$ absorbs polynomial factors and the $O(\log n)$ scale intervals used below. Recall that $$r=n^{-1/3+\delta},\qquad \delta=10^{-4},\qquad k_p=np^3,
 \qquad M_p=(np)^{1/2}.$$ All estimates involving a fixed multiplicative enlargement of the interval of scales have the same meaning, provided its upper endpoint is sufficiently small. In particular, $k_p$ grows as a positive power of $n$ throughout the intervals considered here.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Location of the path and coordinate moments

The following estimate improves the fixed annulus test to a shrinking annulus. It concerns the ordinary observation path averaged under $\mathbf P$; it is not an assertion about every external field.

<a id="stmt-lem-observations-annulus"></a>
<div id="lem:observations-annulus" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:source-7"></a>
**Lemma 9** (The observation annulus). *There are $a,\xi>0$ and a sufficiently small fixed upper scale $p_{\mathrm{top}}>0$ such that $$\label{eq:source-7}
 |\alpha_p(h_p)|\le C k_p^{-a}p^2
 \qquad(r\le p\le p_{\mathrm{top}})$$ outside a set of $\mathbf P$-probability at most $C\exp(-n^\xi)$. On disorder events of probability arbitrarily close to one, the same whole-path statement holds under $Q_\mu$, with a possibly smaller $\xi$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-observations-annulus"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-observations-annulus</summary>

*Proof.* Under $\mathbf P$, the field in spectral coordinates has exactly its spherical distribution. Generate the spherical spin from independent centered Gaussian coordinates $y_i$ of precision $D_i'=d_i+b_n n^{-2/3}$, conditioned on $\sum_i y_i^2=n$; the choice of $b_n$ is that of Proposition <a href="#prop:edge-conditioned-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:edge-conditioned-law">4</a>. Before this conditioning, write $$h_{p,i}=B_{p,i}y_i+B_{p,i}^{1/2}z_i,
 \qquad m=p^2+b_n n^{-2/3},$$ where the $z_i$ are independent standard normals. On an active coordinate $B_{p,i}=m-D_i'$, and direct expansion gives $$\mathbf E_0\bigl(\|h_p\|^2-m^2\|y\|^2\bigr)
 =-m^2\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}R_{p,b_n n^{-2/3}}.$$ Here $\mathbf E_0$ denotes the unconditioned Gaussian expectation. The subtraction of $m^2\|y\|^2$ removes the large contribution of the leading coordinates: its coefficient on the standardized $y_i^2$ term is $(B_{p,i}^2-m^2)/D_i'=D_i'-2m=O(p^2)$.

As a quadratic form in the standardized $(y,z)$ variables, the centered quantity has squared Frobenius norm at most $Cp^4k_p$ and operator norm at most $Cp^2k_p^{1/3}$. Indeed the cross coefficients are $O(p^3/\sqrt{D_i'})$, while the spectral estimates give $$\min_iD_i'\ge c n^{-2/3},\qquad
 \sum_{d_i\le p^2}(D_i')^{-1}\le Cnp.$$ The inactive coordinates are bounded by the capped second resolvent trace. The Gaussian quadratic-form exponential-moment formula therefore bounds deviations larger than $p^2k_p^{9/10}$ by $C\exp(-k_p^{a_0})$ for some $a_0>0$.

This estimate survives conditioning on $\|y\|^2=n$. To see this directly, use the same exponential tilt in the Markov bound, with its parameter at most one eighth of the inverse operator norm. The tilted joint Gaussian covariance, and hence its $y$-marginal covariance, remain comparable to the original ones. A fixed number of leading covariance eigenvalues bounds the density of $n^{-2/3}\|y\|^2$ from above. Its original density at $n^{1/3}$ is bounded below on the chosen spectral set, by Proposition <a href="#prop:edge-conditioned-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:edge-conditioned-law">4</a>. The ratio of norm densities therefore costs only a constant.

After conditioning, the deterministic target for $\|h_p\|^2$ is $\ell_p(b_n n^{-2/3})^2\asymp p^2k_p$. The deviation just proved is a relative $O(k_p^{-1/10})$ error, so it first places the inferred saddle inside any fixed surrounding annulus for all sufficiently large $n$. On that annulus, the derivative of $\ell_p(\alpha)^2$ is comparable to $k_p$. Consequently the preceding deviation bound implies $|\alpha_p-b_n n^{-2/3}|\le Cp^2k_p^{-1/10}$. Since $b_n n^{-2/3}/p^2=O(k_p^{-2/3})$, this proves the fixed-scale assertion.

For the whole path take a sufficiently fine inverse-polynomial mesh in $p^2$. The functions $B_p$, the saddle targets, and their relevant derivatives have polynomial bounds; the positive-part operation defining $B_p$ is continuous at coordinate activations. Conditional on the spin, each coordinate of the noise has independent Gaussian increments. A maximal Gaussian estimate in every mesh interval, followed by a union bound over coordinates and intervals, makes their oscillation smaller than the polynomial tolerance just used. The fixed-scale exponential estimate absorbs this union bound because $k_r=n^{3\delta}$. Finally, Lemma <a href="#lem:edge-biased-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:edge-biased-law">6</a>, Markov’s inequality, and $L_\circ\ge1/2$ outside a vanishing disorder exception transfer the result to $Q_\mu$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:source-8"></a>
The signal-plus-noise representation also gives the moment estimates needed to test the field against individual edge coordinates. Every $u_{p,a}$ has moments of every fixed order bounded uniformly in $a,p$ under $\mathbf P$. For each fixed finite set of leading indices and every fixed $j<\infty$, $$\label{eq:source-8}
 \|u_{p,a}-x_a\|_{L^j(\mathbf P)}
 \le \frac{C_j}{pn^{1/3}},\qquad r\le p\le p_{\mathrm{top}}.$$ Indeed the signal is $(B_{p,a}/p^2)x_a$ and the noise standard deviation is at most $(pn^{1/3})^{-1}$. For a leading coordinate, $|d_a|/p^2=O((pn^{1/3})^{-2})$. The same estimates hold in $L^j(Q_\mu)$ with tight disorder-dependent constants, simultaneously in $p$ in the sense of moment bounds at each scale: apply the signal-plus-noise formula to the tight quenched moments of $x_a$ furnished by the biased first moment and the concentration of $L_\circ$.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Two replicas and the smallest scale

In the next lemma the field is deterministic in spectral coordinates. Consequently $E_U$ means an independent Haar average at that fixed field, whereas $\mathbf E$ subsequently averages a random field with its spherical observation distribution.

<a id="stmt-lem-observations-replicas"></a>
<div id="lem:observations-replicas" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:source-9"></a>
<a id="eq:source-10"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Replica comparison). *Fix a bounded range for $\|h\|/(p^2M_p)$ and take the upper scale sufficiently small. Uniformly for fields in this range, $$\label{eq:source-9}
 E_U L_p(h)^2\le \exp(C+Cnp^4).$$ For a sufficiently large fixed $C_1$, the contribution to this normalized second moment of cube pairs satisfying $|n^{-1}X\cdot X'|>C_1p$ is at most $C\exp(-a k_p)$. At $p=r$, the stronger estimates $$\label{eq:source-10}
 \begin{split}
 E_U(L_r-1)^2&\le Cn^{-1/4},\\
 E_U\!\left[L_r^2\|m_r-m_r^{\mathrm{sp}}\|^2\right]
 &\le C M_r^2 n^{-1/4}
 \end{split}$$ hold for the full mean vectors, written in spectral coordinates. The first bound also holds for the uncapped zero-field likelihood $L_\circ$. The pair-tail assertion at scale $r$ also holds in that uncapped case.*

</div>
