 B=B_S(z)=M(Y,Y),\quad d=(1+B)^{1/2}.$$ The feature coordinates are contracted in every form and dual pairing. Expand $M(u)=\sum_{a\ge0}u^aM_a$ and $b(u)=b_0+ub_1$; the indices $a$ here denote Taylor coefficients, not sites. The quantities that measure the effect of the pair are <a id="eq:comparison-sensitivities"></a>$$\label{eq:comparison-sensitivities}
 \Gamma_e=\frac{T}{d^2}\sum_{k\in e}\mu[w_k|D_kY|^2],\qquad
 R_e=\frac1d\|b_1-M_1Y\|_*.$$ We use $\operatorname{avg}_e$ for the average over the $\binom n2$ pairs. Counting each site in $n-1$ pairs gives $$\label{eq:comparison-energy-average}
 0\le\Gamma_e\le1,\qquad
 \operatorname{avg}_e\Gamma_e
 =\frac2n\frac{T\mathcal E(Y)}{1+B}\le\frac2n.$$

Gaussian and sign entries have the same first three moments, so the comparison uses a fourth-order Taylor remainder. The derivative estimates below will require fourth-power averages of $R_e$.

<a id="stmt-lem-comparison-residual"></a>
<div id="lem:comparison-residual" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:source-44"></a>
<a id="eq:source-45"></a>
**Lemma 40** (Residual estimates). *Uniformly over the disorder and the pair $e$, $$\label{eq:source-44}
 R_e^2\le\frac{2T}{z}\Gamma_e.$$ For $S=S^{[2]}$ one also has $R_e^2\le C_z$, whereas for $S=S^{[1]}$, $$\label{eq:source-45}
 R_e^2\le C_z\bigl(1+n^{1/3}\sqrt{B_n^{[2]}(z)}\bigr).$$ Consequently $\operatorname{avg}_eR_e^2\le C_z n^{-1/3}$. Its fourth-power analogue is $\operatorname{avg}_eR_e^4\le C_z n^{-1/3}$ for $S^{[2]}$, and <a id="eq:source-46"></a>$$\label{eq:source-46}
 \operatorname{avg}_eR_e^4
 \le C_z\bigl(n^{-1/3}+\sqrt{B_n^{[2]}(z)}\bigr)
 \qquad\text{for }S^{[1]}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-comparison-residual"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-comparison-residual</summary>

<a id="eq:comparison-endpoint-multiplier"></a>
<a id="eq:comparison-coefficient-locality"></a>
*Proof.* For a site $k\notin e$, the energy multiplier in $M(u)$ is $1+uq$. At an endpoint $k\in e$, let $s$ be the other spin in the pair. Its conditional mean changes from $t_k$ to $(t_k+us)/(1+ust_k)$, while the marginal measure on $X_{-k}$ acquires the factor $1+ust_k$. Thus its energy term has the multiplier $$\label{eq:comparison-endpoint-multiplier}
 \frac{1-u^2}{1+ust_k}$$ relative to $\mu[w_kD_kf\cdot D_kg]$. Since $|t_k|\le1$, these formulas show that $\|M_a\|_{M\to M^*}\le C^a$ and, for $a\ge2$, that $M_a$ uses only the two endpoint energies. In particular, $$\label{eq:comparison-coefficient-locality}
 \begin{aligned}
 \|M_aY\|_*&\le C^a d\sqrt{\Gamma_e},&
 |M_a(Y,Y)|&\le C^a d^2\Gamma_e\quad(a\ge2),\\
 \|M_1Y\|_*&\le C\sqrt B.
 \end{aligned}$$

Write $E_kg=\mu[g\mid X_{-k}]$. Testing the equation $MY=b_0$ against $qg$ gives the exact identity <a id="eq:comparison-residual-identity"></a>$$\label{eq:comparison-residual-identity}
 (b_1-M_1Y)(g)
  =T\sum_{k\in e}\mu[w_kD_kY\cdot sE_kg].$$ To verify it, the mass terms and all sites outside $e$ cancel. At an endpoint, $D_k(qg)=s(g(X_k=1)+g(X_k=-1))/2$, whereas the first coefficient in <a href="#eq:comparison-endpoint-multiplier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-endpoint-multiplier">[eq:comparison-endpoint-multiplier]</a> is $-st_k$. The remaining sum is $sE_kg$. Cauchy–Schwarz and conditional Jensen now give $$|(b_1-M_1Y)(g)|^2
 \le d^2\Gamma_e T\sum_{k\in e}\mu[w_k|E_kg|^2]
 \le\frac{2T}{z}d^2\Gamma_e\|g\|_M^2,$$ which proves <a href="#eq:source-44" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-44">[eq:source-44]</a>.

By <a href="#eq:comparison-coefficient-locality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-coefficient-locality">[eq:comparison-coefficient-locality]</a>, $R_e^2\le C_z(1+\|b_1\|_*^2)$. For the second feature, $\|qS^{[2]}\|_{L^2(\mu)}=1$, so $\|b_1\|_*^2\le1/z$. For the first feature, use the stationary heat-bath process at the fixed disorder to write $$\begin{aligned}
 \|b_1\|_*^2
 &=n^{1/3}\int_0^\infty e^{-zs}
   \mathbb E_\mu\!\left[
      q(X_0)q(X_{Ts})\rho(X_0,X_{Ts})\right]\,ds\\
 &\le n^{1/3}\left(\frac1z
      \int_0^\infty e^{-zs}
          \mathbb E_\mu[\rho(X_0,X_{Ts})^2]\,ds\right)^{1/2}
  =n^{1/3}\sqrt{B_n^{[2]}(z)/z}.
\end{aligned}$$ The last equality is the Hilbert–Schmidt feature identity $\langle XX^{\mathsf t}/n,X'X'^{\mathsf t}/n\rangle=\rho(X,X')^2$. This proves <a href="#eq:source-45" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-45">[eq:source-45]</a>. Finally, <a href="#eq:source-44" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-44">[eq:source-44]</a> and <a href="#eq:comparison-energy-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-energy-average">[eq:comparison-energy-average]</a> give the second-power average. Multiplying it by the respective pointwise bound on $R_e^2$ proves both fourth-power estimates. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:comparison-h"></a>
The residual is small on average because a single pair touches only two site energies. The other contribution to a trace derivative is a correlation with $q$. The static overlap estimate controls precisely this contribution. Define $$\label{eq:comparison-h}
 h_e=|\mu q|+d^{-2}|M_1(Y,Y)|+R_e+\sqrt{\Gamma_e}.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-comparison-average"></a>
<div id="lem:comparison-average" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:comparison-average-second"></a>
<a id="eq:comparison-average-first"></a>
**Lemma 41** (Averaged sensitivities). *For either feature, $\operatorname{avg}_e(h_e+h_e^4)\le C_z$ deterministically. There is a deterministic sequence $\epsilon_n\to0$ such that, uniformly $t\in[0,1]$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_t\operatorname{avg}_e(h_e+h_e^4)&\le\epsilon_n
       &&\text{for }S^{[2]},\label{eq:comparison-average-second}\\
 \mathbb E_t\operatorname{avg}_e(h_e+h_e^4)&\le\epsilon_n+
             C_z\mathbb E_t\sqrt{B_n^{[2]}(z)}
       &&\text{for }S^{[1]}.
       \label{eq:comparison-average-first}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-comparison-average"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-comparison-average</summary>

*Proof.* The first two terms in <a href="#eq:comparison-h" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-h">[eq:comparison-h]</a> are bounded by constants. For the second of them introduce the nonnegative function $$p_Y=\frac{z|Y|^2+T\sum_k w_k|D_kY|^2}{d^2}.$$ Its mass is $B/(1+B)\le1$. The Markov resolvent representation yields $\|Y\|_\infty\le\|S\|_\infty/z$, with vector norm intended, and hence $\|p_Y\|_\infty\le C_z n^2$ for the first feature and $\|p_Y\|_\infty\le C_z n^{5/3}$ for the second. The endpoint formula gives $$\left|d^{-2}M_1(Y,Y)-\mu[qp_Y]\right|\le2\Gamma_e.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-overlap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-overlap">39</a>, first with $p=1$ and then with $p=p_Y$, therefore shows that these first two terms have vanishing pair averages in expectation, uniformly in $t$. Their fourth powers have the same property because the terms are bounded. The remaining terms are controlled by <a href="#eq:comparison-energy-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-energy-average">[eq:comparison-energy-average]</a>, Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-residual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-residual">40</a>, and Cauchy–Schwarz: $$\operatorname{avg}_e R_e\le C_z n^{-1/6},\qquad
 \operatorname{avg}_e\sqrt{\Gamma_e}\le\sqrt{2/n}.$$ Combining these estimates proves <a href="#eq:comparison-average-second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-average-second">[eq:comparison-average-second]</a> and <a href="#eq:comparison-average-first" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-average-first">[eq:comparison-average-first]</a>. The deterministic bound follows from the same estimates and $B_n^{[2]}(z)\le1/z$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### Fourth derivatives of bounded trace observables

The first trace need not be bounded uniformly in $n$. We compare it through the bounded coordinate $(1+B)^{-1}$. The next lemma turns the preceding sensitivities into the derivative estimate needed for replacement.

<a id="stmt-lem-comparison-derivative"></a>
<div id="lem:comparison-derivative" class="lemma">

**Lemma 42**. *Fix finitely many traces $B_a=B_n^{[k_a]}(z_a)$, with $k_a\in\{1,2\}$ and $z_a>0$, and a smooth function $F$ on a neighborhood of $[0,1]^m$. For an arbitrary base disorder and pair $e$, let $B_a(\theta)$ denote the traces after adding $\theta$ to the interaction coefficient of $e$, and let $\mathfrak h_e=\sum_{a=1}^m h_{e,a}$, with each sensitivity computed at the base disorder. Then for all sufficiently large $n$, <a id="eq:comparison-fourth-derivative"></a>$$\label{eq:comparison-fourth-derivative}
 \sup_{|\theta|\le2\log n/\sqrt n}
 \left|\frac{d^4}{d\theta^4}
 F\left(\frac1{1+B_1(\theta)},\ldots,
        \frac1{1+B_m(\theta)}\right)\right|
 \le C_F(\mathfrak h_e+\mathfrak h_e^4).$$ At every real base disorder, derivatives of orders at most four also have a deterministic bound polynomial in $n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-comparison-derivative"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-comparison-derivative</summary>

*Proof.* We first consider one trace. Complexify the finite-dimensional form space, extending the forms and dual pairings complex bilinearly. The coefficient bounds for $M(u)$ imply that $M^{-1/2}M(u)M^{-1/2}$ differs from the identity in norm by $O(|u|)$ on a fixed disk. Its inverse is therefore analytic and uniformly bounded on a smaller fixed disk. Put $r(u)=b(u)-M(u)Y$ and $A(u)=b(u)M(u)^{-1}b(u)$. Completing the quadratic expression around $Y$ gives the algebraic identity $$A(u)=2b(u)Y-M(u)(Y,Y)+r(u)M(u)^{-1}r(u).$$ The coefficient of $u$ in $(A(u)-B)/d^2$ is $$\frac{M_1(Y,Y)+2(b_1-M_1Y)(Y)}{d^2},$$ whose absolute value is at most $Ch_e$. For higher coefficients, <a href="#eq:comparison-coefficient-locality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-coefficient-locality">[eq:comparison-coefficient-locality]</a> gives $$\frac{\|r_1\|_*}{d}=R_e,\qquad
 \frac{\|r_a\|_*}{d}\le C^a\sqrt{\Gamma_e}\quad(a\ge2).$$ The direct term $-M_a(Y,Y)/d^2$ is at most $C^a\Gamma_e$. Coefficients of the residual quadratic term are bounded, using the analytic inverse, by $C^a(R_e^2+R_e\sqrt{\Gamma_e}+\Gamma_e)\le C^ah_e^2$. Thus every coefficient of order $a\ge2$ in $(A(u)-B)/d^2$ is bounded by $C^ah_e^2$.

The actual trace divides $A(u)$ by the total mass $1+u\mu q$. Thus, with $$\delta(u)=\frac{B(u)-B}{d^2},$$ its linear coefficient is $O(h_e)$ and its coefficients of order $a\ge2$ are bounded by $C^a(h_e+h_e^2)$. From <a href="#eq:source-45" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-45">[eq:source-45]</a> and <a href="#eq:comparison-trivial-traces" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-trivial-traces">[eq:comparison-trivial-traces]</a>, $h_e\le C_z(1+n^{1/6})$ for the first feature; for the second it is bounded. Consequently $|u|h_e=o(1)$ uniformly for $|u|\le2\log n/\sqrt n$. Summing the coefficient bounds on this interval gives $$\sup|\delta(u)|=o(1),\qquad
 |\delta'(u)|\le Ch_e,\qquad
 |\delta^{(a)}(u)|\le C(h_e+h_e^2),\quad 2\le a\le4.$$ Since $$\frac1{1+B(u)}=\frac1{d^2}\frac1{1+\delta(u)},$$ the chain rule bounds its fourth derivative, and that of any fixed smooth test of it, by $C(h_e+h_e^4)$. The derivatives of $u=\tanh\theta$ through order four are bounded on the interval in question, so the same estimate holds for interaction displacement $\theta$.

For several traces apply the multivariable chain rule and bound every mixed product using $\mathfrak h_e+\mathfrak h_e^4$. Finally, at any real base point the coefficient argument applies at $\theta=0$, where the denominator of the compactification is at least one. The bound $h_e\le C(1+n^{1/6})$ supplies the claimed polynomial bounds, uniformly in that base point. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### Replacement and the two-feature argument

We have obtained a small average fourth derivative under a complete iid mixture disorder. Taylor replacement removes an entry before expanding, so its expansion point does not itself have that law. The following independent completion lets us use the averaged estimates without changing the Taylor coefficients.

<a id="eq:comparison-mixture-derivative"></a>
Let $$\Phi(J)=F\left((1+B_n^{[k_1]}(z_1))^{-1},\ldots,
               (1+B_n^{[k_m]}(z_m))^{-1}\right).$$ Differentiating the finite product mixture measure gives exactly $$\label{eq:comparison-mixture-derivative}
 \frac d{dt}\mathbb E_t\Phi
  =\sum_e\mathbb E_{t,-e}\!\left[
       \mathbb E_{\xi}\Phi(J_{-e},\xi)
       -\mathbb E_g\Phi(J_{-e},g)\right],$$ where $\xi$ is a symmetric sign, $g$ is standard normal, and $\mathbb E_{t,-e}$ averages all entries other than $e$. For fixed $J_{-e}$, Taylor-expand $x\mapsto\Phi(J_{-e},x)$ at zero through degree three. These polynomial terms cancel in <a href="#eq:comparison-mixture-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-mixture-derivative">[eq:comparison-mixture-derivative]</a>, since $\xi$ and $g$ have the same first three moments.

<a id="eq:comparison-remainder"></a>
Independently of both proposed values, draw $J_e^0$ with law $\nu_t$, and write $J^0=(J_{-e},J_e^0)$. If $|x|,|J_e^0|\le\log n$, every point of the Taylor segment between $0$ and $x$ differs from $J_e^0$ by at most $2\log n$. Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-derivative">42</a>, with $J^0$ as its base, therefore bounds the Taylor remainder by $$\label{eq:comparison-remainder}
 C_F\frac{|x|^4}{n^2}
       \bigl(\mathfrak h_e(J^0)+\mathfrak h_e(J^0)^4\bigr).$$ The factor $n^{-2}$ changes four derivatives in the interaction coefficient $J_e/\sqrt n$ into four derivatives in $J_e$. The factor $|x|^4$ is measured from the Taylor center $0$. The independently completed entry is used only to bound the derivatives along the segment, so it does not enter this factor.

<a id="eq:comparison-master-bound"></a>
The events $|g|>\log n$ and $|J_e^0|>\log n$ have probability smaller than every fixed inverse power of $n$, uniformly in $t$. The boundedness of $\Phi$, together with the polynomial derivative bounds in Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-derivative">42</a>, bounds the Taylor polynomial and its remainder by a polynomial in $n$ times $1+|x|^4$. Gaussian tail moments thus make the omitted contributions $o(n^{-2})$, uniformly in $J_{-e}$ and $t$. On the remaining event, enlarge the right side of <a href="#eq:comparison-remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-remainder">[eq:comparison-remainder]</a> by removing its indicator. Its $|x|^4$ factor is independent of the completed base $J^0$ and has bounded expectation. Moreover, if $e$ is chosen uniformly, $J^0$ has the full iid mixture law independently of $e$. Summing the remainders in <a href="#eq:comparison-mixture-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-mixture-derivative">[eq:comparison-mixture-derivative]</a> therefore yields $$\label{eq:comparison-master-bound}
 \left|\frac d{dt}\mathbb E_t\Phi\right|
 \le C_F\mathbb E_t\operatorname{avg}_e
             (\mathfrak h_e+\mathfrak h_e^4)+o(1),$$ uniformly for $0\le t\le1$.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-comparison-derivative-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-comparison-derivative</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:comparison-universality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:comparison-universality">38</a>.* First use only second-feature traces in <a href="#eq:comparison-master-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-master-bound">[eq:comparison-master-bound]</a>. For finitely many traces, $\mathfrak h_e+\mathfrak h_e^4\le C_m\sum_a(h_{e,a}+h_{e,a}^4)$. Equation <a href="#eq:comparison-average-second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-average-second">[eq:comparison-average-second]</a> therefore makes the mixture derivative tend to zero uniformly in $t$. In particular, for one $z$, choose the test $F(v)=1-v$. The Gaussian convergence <a href="#eq:source-43" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-43">[eq:source-43]</a> and the bound $B_n^{[2]}(z)\le1/z$ imply $$\sup_t\mathbb E_t\frac{B_n^{[2]}(z)}{1+B_n^{[2]}(z)}\longrightarrow0.$$ Since $b\le(1+1/z)b/(1+b)$ for $0\le b\le1/z$, this proves the first assertion of the theorem and, by Jensen, $\sup_t\mathbb E_t\sqrt{B_n^{[2]}(z)}\to0$.

Now use first-feature traces. Equation <a href="#eq:comparison-average-first" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-average-first">[eq:comparison-average-first]</a> makes their right side in <a href="#eq:comparison-master-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-master-bound">[eq:comparison-master-bound]</a> tend to zero uniformly in $t$. Integrating from $t=0$ to $t=1$ shows equality of the limiting expectations of every smooth test of finitely many compactified traces. Such tests determine probability laws on $[0,1]^m$. By Proposition <a href="#prop:trace-goe" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:trace-goe">34</a>, the Gaussian trace vector converges to the finite vector in <a href="#eq:source-40" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-40">[eq:source-40]</a>; its compactification therefore has all coordinates strictly positive almost surely. The continuous mapping theorem for the inverse map $v\mapsto v^{-1}-1$ recovers the same joint limit for the sign traces. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:functional"></a>
### The functional limit and its randomness

Proposition <a href="#prop:trace-goe" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:trace-goe">34</a> and Theorem <a href="#thm:comparison-universality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:comparison-universality">38</a> identify the same finite collections of resolvent traces for Gaussian and sign disorder. We first turn these statements into convergence of autocorrelation functions. We then prove decay and show that the edge dependence produces a random, rather than deterministic, limit.

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
#### From resolvents to continuous functions

Let $E_n$ be the spectral resolution of the nonnegative operator $TH$, and define the finite measure $$\nu_n(I)=\sum_{i=1}^n
 \langle n^{-1/3}X_i,E_n(I)n^{-1/3}X_i\rangle_\mu$$ for Borel sets $I\subset[0,\infty)$. Basis invariance gives $$B_n(z)=\int\frac{d\nu_n(\lambda)}{z+\lambda},
 \qquad
 A_{n,J}(s)=\int e^{-s\lambda}\,d\nu_n(\lambda).$$ Although $\nu_n([0,\infty))=n^{1/3}$ diverges, its division by $1+\lambda$ has tight mass. This is the reason to use resolvents rather than the unweighted spectral measures.

<a id="stmt-lem-functional-spectral"></a>
<div id="lem:functional-spectral" class="lemma">

**Lemma 43** (A continuity principle for spectral measures). *Let $\nu_n$ and $\nu$ be random nonnegative measures on $[0,\infty)$ such that their integrals against $(1+\lambda)^{-1}$ are finite almost surely, for every $n$ and for $\nu$. Suppose the corresponding integrals against $(z+\lambda)^{-1}$ converge jointly in law for every finite collection of positive $z$, including $z=1$. Then $$\left(s\longmapsto\int e^{-s\lambda}\,d\nu_n(\lambda)\right)
 \ \Longrightarrow\
 \left(s\longmapsto\int e^{-s\lambda}\,d\nu(\lambda)\right)$$ in $C_{\rm loc}((0,\infty))$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-functional-spectral"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-functional-spectral</summary>

*Proof.* Set $d\eta_n=(1+\lambda)^{-1}d\nu_n$ and regard $\eta_n$ as a finite measure on the compact space $K=[0,\infty]$, giving no mass to infinity. Its total mass is the Stieltjes transform at $z=1$, and is therefore tight. Finite measures on $K$ with bounded total mass form a compact set, so the laws of $\eta_n$ are tight.

For $z>0$ the function $$k_z(\lambda)=\frac{1+\lambda}{z+\lambda},\qquad k_z(\infty)=1,$$ is continuous on $K$. A countable dense family of these functions, including $k_1=1$, determines a finite measure even when that measure has mass at infinity. Indeed their closed linear span contains $1$ and $r_z(\lambda)=(z+\lambda)^{-1}$, with $r_z(\infty)=0$. The partial-fraction identity $r_zr_w=(r_z-r_w)/(w-z)$, followed by uniform limits when $w=z$, shows that this span is an algebra. It separates the points of $K$, so it is dense in $C(K)$. Consequently every subsequential law is the law of $d\eta=(1+\lambda)^{-1}d\nu$, with no mass at infinity. Here joint convergence on the countable determining family identifies the law of the random measure, rather than only its mean.

For $s>0$, define $h_s(\lambda)=(1+\lambda)e^{-s\lambda}$ and $h_s(\infty)=0$. On each compact interval $a\leq s\leq b$, $a>0$, the map $s\mapsto h_s$ is continuous in the uniform norm on $K$. Thus weak convergence of finite measures on $K$ gives convergence of their $h_s$ integrals uniformly on $[a,b]$: a finite uniform-norm net of the functions $h_s$ reduces the assertion to finitely many continuous tests, while weak convergence bounds the total masses. The resulting map from measures to $C_{\rm loc}((0,\infty))$ is continuous. The continuous mapping theorem proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-functional-identification"></a>
<div id="prop:functional-identification" class="proposition">

**Proposition 44** (Identification of the functional limit). *The series <a href="#eq:source-2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-2">[eq:source-2]</a> has a Borel version $g\mapsto\mathcal A_g$ with values in $C_{\rm loc}((0,\infty))$. For both Gaussian and symmetric-sign entries, $$\operatorname{Law}(A_{n,J})\Longrightarrow
 Q_{\rm crit}=\operatorname{Law}(\mathcal A_g),$$ where the same edge law and the same deterministic coefficient $c_*$ are used in <a href="#eq:source-2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-2">[eq:source-2]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-functional-identification"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-functional-identification</summary>

*Proof.* For a fixed edge configuration $g$, let $E_g$ be the spectral resolution of $c_*H_\Pi$ and put $$\nu_g(I)=\sum_{a\geq1}\langle x_a,E_g(I)x_a\rangle_\Pi.$$ This countable sum defines a nonnegative measure. By Proposition <a href="#prop:trace-goe" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:trace-goe">34</a>, its division by $1+\lambda$ has finite mass $B_g(1)$ almost surely. It follows in particular that the series in <a href="#eq:source-2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-2">[eq:source-2]</a> defines a continuous function $\mathcal A_g$ on $(0,\infty)$.

These constructions are measurable in $g$. The density prescription for $\Pi$ makes each cylinder integral measurable. Resolvent pairings can then be computed by the variational supremum over a countable family of smooth compact cylinders. The family can be chosen independently of $g$ by ordinary approximation of a function and its first derivatives on finite-dimensional compact sets; it is a form core by Proposition <a href="#prop:gradient-domain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gradient-domain">22</a>. Thus the resolvent pairings, the divided spectral measure determined by them, and $\mathcal A_g$ are measurable.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:functional-spectral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:functional-spectral">43</a> to the finite-dimensional trace convergence in Proposition <a href="#prop:trace-goe" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:trace-goe">34</a> and Theorem <a href="#thm:comparison-universality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:comparison-universality">38</a>. For either entry law, $$\operatorname{Law}(A_{n,J})\Longrightarrow
 Q_{\rm crit}:=\operatorname{Law}(\mathcal A_g)
 \quad\text{in }C_{\rm loc}((0,\infty)).$$ The common coefficient is the deterministic $c_*$ of Theorem <a href="#thm:micro-relaxation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:micro-relaxation">33</a>; no further change of time is involved. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### Decay and positive values

The spectral representation makes $\mathcal A_g$ nonnegative and nonincreasing. Proposition <a href="#prop:gradient-domain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gradient-domain">22</a> identifies the kernel of $H_\Pi$ as the constants. Each $x_a$ is centered by sign symmetry of $\Pi$, so $\nu_g$ has no atom at zero. For any fixed $s_0>0$, $e^{-s_0\lambda}$ is integrable against $\nu_g$. Dominated convergence for $s\geq s_0$ therefore gives $$\lim_{s\to\infty}\mathcal A_g(s)=0.$$

<a id="eq:functional-positive"></a>
There is also a useful positive lower bound. Put $v(g)=\Pi[x_1^2]$, which is finite by the coordinate moment bounds and strictly positive by full support of the first-coordinate marginal. The coordinate is in the form domain with energy one, by Proposition <a href="#prop:gradient-domain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gradient-domain">22</a>. Jensen’s inequality for its normalized spectral measure gives $$\mathcal A_g(s)\geq
 \langle x_1,e^{-s c_*H_\Pi}x_1\rangle_\Pi
 \geq v(g)\exp\{-s c_*/v(g)\}>0.
 \label{eq:functional-positive}$$ It remains to show that these functions do not have a deterministic law. For this we will vary a leading edge point while retaining absolute continuity with respect to the original edge law.

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
#### Finite shifts of the edge process

Let $\mathsf P$ denote the law of the increasing edge sequence $g$. For an integer $m$ and a vector $h\in\mathbb R^m$, define $$T_hg=(g_1+h_1,\ldots,g_m+h_m,g_{m+1},\ldots),$$ and let $\mathcal O_h$ be the event that this sequence is still strictly increasing. We use the restriction of $\mathsf P$ to this event, without normalizing its mass.

<a id="stmt-lem-functional-shift"></a>
<div id="lem:functional-shift" class="lemma">

**Lemma 45** (Absolute continuity under finite shifts). *For every fixed $m$ and $h\in\mathbb R^m$, $$(T_h)_*(\mathsf P|_{\mathcal O_h})\ll\mathsf P.$$ In particular, lowering $g_1$ by any fixed positive amount gives a law absolutely continuous with respect to $\mathsf P$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-functional-shift"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-functional-shift</summary>

*Proof.* We prove the claim by finite-dimensional GOE density ratios. Let $\mathsf P_n$ denote the ordered GOE edge law in dimension $n$. When a sequence-valued argument is needed, append the values $g_{n,n}+j$, $j\geq1$, after its final coordinate. This convention does not affect any fixed coordinate for large $n$, so $\mathsf P_n\Longrightarrow\mathsf P$ in the product topology; it also commutes with $T_h$ whenever $n>m$. In the variables $g_{a,n}=n^{2/3}(2-\lambda_a)$, the ordered density is proportional to $$\exp\left\{-\frac n4\sum_{a=1}^n
                 (2-n^{-2/3}g_{a,n})^2\right\}
 \prod_{i<j}(g_{j,n}-g_{i,n}).$$ Translation of the first $m$ variables has Jacobian one. Its Gaussian log-density ratio is $n^{1/3}\sum_{k\leq m}h_k+O(1)$ on sets where the first $m$ coordinates are bounded. For a fixed $k\leq m$, the spectral estimates of Section <a href="#sec:edge-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:edge-law">2</a> give, tightly, $$\sum_{m<i\leq n}\frac1{g_{i,n}-g_{k,n}}=n^{1/3}+O(1),
 \qquad
 \sum_{m<i\leq n}\frac1{(g_{i,n}-g_{k,n})^2}=O(1).$$ To see the first assertion, compare the reciprocals with $(g_{i,n}+b_n)^{-1}$, where $g_{1,n}+b_n\geq1$. Their difference is summable by the square-tail bound, while $n^{1/3}-\sum_i(g_{i,n}+b_n)^{-1}$ is tight. Tight separation of the first finitely many eigenvalues controls the remaining denominators.

The logarithm of the Vandermonde ratio involving indices $k\leq m<i$ contains $$\sum_{m<i\leq n}\log\left(1-\frac{h_k}{g_{i,n}-g_{k,n}}\right).$$ Beyond a sufficiently large fixed index, the fractions have absolute value at most $1/2$. Taylor’s inequality and the preceding two bounds show that this sum is $-h_kn^{1/3}+O(1)$. The finitely many remaining factors, including pairs within the first $m$ coordinates, are bounded away from zero after imposing positive margins on the old and shifted gaps. The linear terms cancel those of the Gaussian factor. Consequently the new-to-old density ratio has a positive lower bound on sets of probability arbitrarily close to one, after any fixed localization inside the strict-order event.

Here is the measure-theoretic direction of the resulting implication. Let $\chi$ be a continuous function of finitely many leading coordinates, with $0\leq\chi\leq1$, supported where the shifted order has a positive margin and the leading coordinates are bounded. For every $\epsilon>0$, the density-ratio bound supplies $c_\epsilon>0$ such that, for every bounded nonnegative continuous function $f$ of the edge sequence, $$\mathsf P_n(f)\geq c_\epsilon
 \left(\mathsf P_n[\chi\,f\circ T_h]
                       -\epsilon\|f\|_\infty\right).$$ The common spectral exceptional sets have probability at most $\epsilon$ for all sufficiently large $n$. Pass to the edge limit. For a compact subset of a $\mathsf P$-null set, take continuous tests between zero and one, equal to one on that compact subset and supported in open sets of arbitrarily small $\mathsf P$-mass. The inequality bounds its $(T_h)_*(\chi\mathsf P)$-mass by $\epsilon$. Inner regularity gives the same bound for every $\mathsf P$-null set. Letting $\epsilon\downarrow0$ proves absolute continuity for this localized measure. Finally exhaust $\mathcal O_h$ by such continuous localizations. This proves the lemma without requiring a uniform bound on the density ratio over the entire edge law. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-functional-randomness"></a>
<div id="prop:functional-randomness" class="proposition">

**Proposition 46** (Randomness of the limit). *For every fixed $s>0$, the finite random variable $\mathcal A_g(s)$ is essentially unbounded. In particular $Q_{\rm crit}$ is not a point mass.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-functional-randomness"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-functional-randomness</summary>

*Proof.* Lower $g_1$ by $t>0$, leaving the other points fixed, and denote the resulting sequence by $g^{(t)}$. Choose the auxiliary parameter $b$ large enough for both Gaussian prescriptions. Only $l_1$ changes, and <a href="#eq:source-1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-1">[eq:source-1]</a> shows $$D_{g^{(t)}}(b)-D_g(b)
 =-\big(l_1^{(t)}-l_1\big).$$ At a common coordinate sequence $x$, the finite centered-square sums in the two prescriptions differ by $-(l_1^{(t)}-l_1)$. Thus, wherever these sums converge, $S_{g^{(t)}}(x)=S_g(x)-(l_1^{(t)}-l_1)$. Consequently $$S_{g^{(t)}}(x)-D_{g^{(t)}}(b)=S_g(x)-D_g(b).$$ The two renormalized constraints therefore coincide. Comparing the first Gaussian coordinate in their conditioning densities gives $$\frac{d\Pi_{g^{(t)}}}{d\Pi_g}(x)
 =\frac{\exp(t x_1^2/2)}{\Pi_g[\exp(t x_1^2/2)]}.$$ The denominator is finite: increase $b$ until $g_1+b>t$ and use the bounded conditional first-coordinate density relative to the centered Gaussian of variance $(g_1+b)^{-1}$. The independence of the conditioned law from $b$ then gives the displayed identity for $\Pi_g$.

For a nonnegative random variable with unbounded support, exponential tilts with parameter tending to infinity have means tending to infinity. Indeed, for any $M$, the mass below $M$ after tilting is at most $e^{-t/2}\Pi_g[x_1^2\leq M]/\Pi_g[x_1^2\geq M+1]$. It follows that $v(g^{(t)})\to\infty$ as $t\to\infty$.

Suppose that $v(g)$ had an essential finite upper bound. By Lemma <a href="#lem:functional-shift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:functional-shift">45</a>, this same bound would hold for $v(g^{(t)})$ almost surely for every positive integer $t$. Intersecting these countably many full-measure events contradicts the preceding divergence. Therefore $v(g)$ is essentially unbounded. The lower bound <a href="#eq:functional-positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functional-positive">[eq:functional-positive]</a> proves the same assertion for $\mathcal A_g(s)$ at every fixed positive $s$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-functional-randomness-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-functional-randomness</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Proposition <a href="#prop:functional-identification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:functional-identification">44</a> proves convergence of the laws in $C_{\rm loc}((0,\infty))$ for both entry distributions and identifies their common limit by <a href="#eq:source-2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-2">[eq:source-2]</a>. The same deterministic $c_*>0$ occurs in both models. The spectral representation and the trivial form kernel prove nonnegativity, monotonicity, and almost-sure decay. Equation <a href="#eq:functional-positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functional-positive">[eq:functional-positive]</a> excludes the zero function, and Proposition <a href="#prop:functional-randomness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:functional-randomness">46</a> establishes sample-to-sample randomness. ◻

</details>

Decay at infinity is not a closed condition in $C_{\rm loc}((0,\infty))$. The almost-sure decay proved above can therefore coexist with nondecaying functions in the closed topological support. Appendix <a href="#sec:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:support">10</a> gives a precise example: the constant-one function belongs to the closed topological support of $Q_{\rm crit}$.

<a id="sec:support"></a>
### The closed topological support

The decay assertion in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> holds almost surely under $Q_{\rm crit}$. The set of functions that decay at infinity is not closed in $C_{\rm loc}((0,\infty))$, and in the present problem the distinction is substantive. We prove that the closed topological support contains a nondecaying function. For an edge configuration $g$, write $\mathcal A_g$ for the function in <a href="#eq:source-2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-2">[eq:source-2]</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-support-constant"></a>
<div id="prop:support-constant" class="proposition">

**Proposition 47**. *The constant function $s\mapsto 1$ belongs to the closed topological support of $Q_{\rm crit}$ in $C_{\rm loc}((0,\infty))$.*

</div>

The proof modifies finitely many edge points so that the conditioned law concentrates near $x_1=\pm1$. A smooth function distinguishing these two regions has vanishing form energy, while the resolvent contribution of the remaining coordinates vanishes. The finite-shift absolute continuity from Lemma <a href="#lem:functional-shift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:functional-shift">45</a> ensures that every function used in the approximation belongs to the closed support.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-support-constant"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-support-constant</summary>

*Proof.* Let $K=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}Q_{\rm crit}$ denote the closed topological support. Since $C_{\rm loc}((0,\infty))$ is separable, $Q_{\rm crit}(K)=1$. Consider all shifts of finitely many edge points by rational amounts, restricted to the event that the shifted points remain strictly ordered. There are countably many such shifts. By Lemma <a href="#lem:functional-shift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:functional-shift">45</a>, outside a single null set, every valid shift $g'$ of $g$ gives a configuration for which the conditioned measure, the form identities, and <a href="#eq:source-2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-2">[eq:source-2]</a> are defined, and for which $\mathcal A_{g'}$ belongs to $K$. We fix such a configuration $g$, also satisfying the spectral bounds of Section <a href="#sec:edge-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:edge-law">2</a>. Choose and fix $b$ with $g_1+b>0$, and put $$l_a=(g_a+b)^{-1},\qquad D=D(b).$$ In particular, $l_a$ is strictly decreasing, $\sum_a l_a=\infty$, and $l_a\le C a^{-2/3}$ for all sufficiently large $a$. All constants below may depend on this fixed configuration and on $b$.

<a id="eq:support-subsequence"></a>
<a id="eq:support-scale"></a>
*Choosing the modified variances.* We leave $g_1$ fixed and modify $g_2,\ldots,g_m$, along a sequence of integers $m\to\infty$. Let $J=60$ and define $$A_m=\sum_{i=2}^m l_i+D+l_1-1.$$ Then $A_m\to\infty$ and $A_m=O(m^{1/3})$. There are arbitrarily large $m$ such that $$\label{eq:support-subsequence}
 l_{m+1}\le \frac{A_m}{2m}.$$ Indeed, if the reverse strict inequality held eventually, the identity $A_{m+1}=A_m+l_{m+1}$ would imply $A_{m+1}>A_m(1+1/(2m))$, and hence $A_m\ge c m^{1/2}$, contrary to the upper bound. Restrict henceforth to integers satisfying <a href="#eq:support-subsequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-subsequence">[eq:support-subsequence]</a>, and set $M=A_m/(m-1)$. For all sufficiently large such integers, $$\label{eq:support-scale}
 \frac{c}{m}\le M\le C m^{-2/3}.$$

<a id="eq:support-separation"></a>
<a id="eq:support-square-sum"></a>
Choose strictly decreasing positive numbers on indices $2,\ldots,m$ whose sum is $A_m$, with the first $J-1$ numbers close to $2M$ and the others close to $M$. This is possible by adjusting the latter group by a relative amount $O(J/m)$ and then making small distinct perturbations of zero total sum. For large $m$ these numbers are less than $l_1$ and greater than $l_{m+1}$, by <a href="#eq:support-subsequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-subsequence">[eq:support-subsequence]</a>. They therefore define an ordered modification of the edge points through $g_i'=1/l_i'-b$. Approximate each of the finitely many shifts $g_i'-g_i$ by a rational number, sufficiently closely to retain strict order and all the strict comparisons just imposed. We continue to write $g^{(m)}$ and $l_a^{(m)}$ for the resulting configuration and variances. The approximation can also be chosen so that the following bounds hold, with constants independent of $m$: <a id="eq:support-variances"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 &l_1^{(m)}=l_1,\qquad l_a^{(m)}=l_a\quad(a>m),
       \qquad l_a^{(m)}\asymp M\quad(2\le a\le m),
       \label{eq:support-variances}\\
 &\frac{l_a^{(m)}}{l_j^{(m)}}\le q<1
       \quad(a>J,\ 1\le j\le J),
       \label{eq:support-separation}\\
 &V_m:=D^{(m)}(b)+l_1\longrightarrow1,
       \qquad
       \sum_{a>1}(l_a^{(m)})^2\le C m^{-1/3}.
       \label{eq:support-square-sum}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $D^{(m)}$ is the value of <a href="#eq:source-1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-1">[eq:source-1]</a> for $g^{(m)}$. To check the first conclusion in <a href="#eq:support-square-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-square-sum">[eq:support-square-sum]</a>, the finite-change identity from that equation gives $$D^{(m)}(b)-D(b)
   =-\sum_{i=2}^m(l_i^{(m)}-l_i).$$ Before rational approximation our choice of sum makes $V_m=1$ exactly; afterwards we can require $|V_m-1|\le m^{-1}$. The square-sum bound follows from $mM^2\le C m^{-1/3}$ and $\sum_{a>m}l_a^2\le C m^{-1/3}$. By the choice of the original configuration, each $\mathcal A_{g^{(m)}}$ belongs to $K$.

<a id="eq:support-tail-density"></a>
*Concentration after conditioning.* Let $\gamma_m$ be the product law of centered normals on indices $a>1$ with variances $l_a^{(m)}$, and let $$S_m=\sum_{a>1}\bigl(x_a^2-l_a^{(m)}\bigr).$$ This series converges in $L^2(\gamma_m)$, and <a href="#eq:support-square-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-square-sum">[eq:support-square-sum]</a> implies $S_m\to0$ in probability under $\gamma_m$. Write $\Pi_m=\Pi_{g^{(m)}}$. Eliminating $x_1$ in the conditioning prescription shows that the $a>1$ marginal of $\Pi_m$ has density $$\label{eq:support-tail-density}
 \begin{aligned}
 r_m&=Z_m^{-1}h_m(S_m),\qquad Z_m=\mathbb E_{\gamma_m}h_m(S_m),\\
 h_m(v)&=\mathbf 1_{\{v<V_m\}}(V_m-v)^{-1/2}
             \exp\left(-\frac{V_m-v}{2l_1}\right),
 \end{aligned}$$ relative to $\gamma_m$. Under the full law, $x_1^2=V_m-S_m$, and the sign of $x_1$ is independent and symmetric.

<a id="eq:support-mgf"></a>
<a id="eq:support-density-bound"></a>
We give bounds near the singularity in <a href="#eq:support-tail-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-tail-density">[eq:support-tail-density]</a>, because concentration under the product law alone would not suffice. Put $t_m=m^{1/10}$. For either sign, the centered Gaussian square-sum formula and <a href="#eq:support-square-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-square-sum">[eq:support-square-sum]</a> give $$\label{eq:support-mgf}
 \log\mathbb E_{\gamma_m}e^{\pm t_m S_m}
 \le C t_m^2\sum_{a>1}(l_a^{(m)})^2\le C,$$ since $t_m\max_{a>1}l_a^{(m)}\to0$. Under either exponential tilt the coordinates remain independent normals, with variances $l_a^{(m)}/(1\mp2t_m l_a^{(m)})$, comparable to the original variances. The sum of the squares of any fixed four coordinates among $2,\ldots,J$ has density bounded by $C/M$: its characteristic function has absolute value at most $C(1+M^2u^2)^{-1}$, whose integral is $O(M^{-1})$. Convolution with the remaining coordinates does not increase this bound. The same assertion holds for the infinite sum by its $L^2$ convergence. Undoing the tilt and using <a href="#eq:support-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-scale">[eq:support-scale]</a>–<a href="#eq:support-mgf" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-mgf">[eq:support-mgf]</a>, we obtain a density $p_m$ of $S_m$ satisfying $$\label{eq:support-density-bound}
 p_m(v)\le C m e^{-t_m|v|},\qquad v\in\mathbb R.$$

<a id="eq:support-density-norm"></a>
<a id="eq:support-concentration"></a>
<a id="eq:support-coordinate-moments"></a>
The factors $h_m(v)$ are uniformly bounded on the complement of a fixed neighborhood of $v=V_m$. Near that point, which stays bounded away from zero, <a href="#eq:support-density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-density-bound">[eq:support-density-bound]</a> supplies an exponentially small factor, while the singularity $(V_m-v)^{-p/2}$ is integrable for every $p<2$. Consequently, concentration of $S_m$ at zero gives $$\label{eq:support-density-norm}
 Z_m\longrightarrow e^{-1/(2l_1)},\qquad
 \sup_m\|r_m\|_{L^{3/2}(\gamma_m)}<\infty.$$ The same estimate, integrated over $|S_m|>\varepsilon$, shows that for each fixed $\varepsilon>0$ there are positive constants $C_\varepsilon,c_\varepsilon$ such that $$\label{eq:support-concentration}
 \Pi_m\{|x_1^2-1|>\varepsilon\}
       \le C_\varepsilon e^{-c_\varepsilon m^{1/10}}$$ for all sufficiently large $m$. Integrating the negative tail of $S_m$ also gives convergence of $x_1^2$ to $1$ in every fixed moment. Finally, Hölder’s inequality applied to <a href="#eq:support-density-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-density-norm">[eq:support-density-norm]</a> yields, for each fixed $p<\infty$, $$\label{eq:support-coordinate-moments}
 \sup_{m}\sup_{a>1}
 \Pi_m\left[\left|x_a/\sqrt{l_a^{(m)}}\right|^p\right]<\infty.$$

*The first coordinate and the trace tail.* Write $H_m=H_{\Pi_m}$ and $\mathcal E_m(F)=\int|\nabla F|^2\,d\Pi_m$. Choose an odd smooth compactly supported function $\phi$ on $\mathbb R$ which equals $1$ near $1$ and $-1$ near $-1$. The preceding concentration and moment estimates imply $$\|x_1-\phi(x_1)\|_{L^2(\Pi_m)}\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \|\phi(x_1)\|_{L^2(\Pi_m)}^2\longrightarrow1,\qquad
 \mathcal E_m(\phi(x_1))\longrightarrow0.$$ For $0\le s\le S$, the spectral theorem gives $$0\le \|\phi(x_1)\|_2^2
       -\langle\phi(x_1),e^{-s c_*H_m}\phi(x_1)\rangle
       \le S c_*\mathcal E_m(\phi(x_1)).$$ Semigroup contraction therefore shows that the first coordinate’s contribution to $\mathcal A_{g^{(m)}}(s)$ tends to $1$, uniformly for $s$ in bounded intervals. Coordinates $2,\ldots,J$ contribute at most $\sum_{a=2}^J\Pi_m[x_a^2]=O(M)$, which tends to zero. It remains to control the infinitely many coordinates beyond $J$. We use the angular integration-by-parts estimate of Section <a href="#sec:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:trace">7</a>. Here the angular identity <a href="#eq:source-23" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-23">[eq:source-23]</a> is exact, so there is no likelihood-score error. The remaining task is to make the coefficient and inverse-moment bounds uniform in $m$.

<a id="eq:support-inverse-moments"></a>
Let $R_0=\sum_{j=1}^Jx_j^2$. We first establish the uniform inverse moments needed for angular integration by parts. By <a href="#eq:support-density-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-density-norm">[eq:support-density-norm]</a>, Hölder’s inequality, and $R_0\ge\sum_{j=2}^Jx_j^2$, $$\Pi_m[R_0^{-8}]
 \le C\left(\mathbb E_{\gamma_m}
       \left[\left(\sum_{j=2}^Jx_j^2\right)^{-24}\right]\right)^{1/3}
 \le C M^{-8}\le C m^8.$$ The Gaussian inverse moment is finite because $J-1=59>48$. On $\{x_1^2\ge1/2\}$ the quantity $R_0^{-4}$ is bounded by $16$; on the complement, Cauchy–Schwarz and <a href="#eq:support-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-concentration">[eq:support-concentration]</a> absorb the preceding polynomial bound. Thus $$\label{eq:support-inverse-moments}
 \sup_m\Pi_m[R_0^{-4}]<\infty.$$

<a id="eq:support-field-norms"></a>
For $a>J$ put $$\delta_{aj}=(l_a^{(m)})^{-1}-(l_j^{(m)})^{-1},\qquad
 \mathcal V_a=\sum_{j=1}^J\frac{x_j}{R_0\delta_{aj}}
                   (x_j\partial_a-x_a\partial_j).$$ By <a href="#eq:support-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-separation">[eq:support-separation]</a>, $\delta_{aj}\ge(1-q)/l_a^{(m)}$. The calculation in <a href="#eq:source-42" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-42">[eq:source-42]</a>, now in the limiting coordinates, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 |\mathcal V_a|
   &\le C l_a^{(m)}(1+|x_a|/\sqrt{R_0}),\\
 |\operatorname{div}\mathcal V_a|
   &\le C l_a^{(m)}|x_a|/R_0.
\end{aligned}$$ Together with <a href="#eq:support-coordinate-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-coordinate-moments">[eq:support-coordinate-moments]</a> and <a href="#eq:support-inverse-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-inverse-moments">[eq:support-inverse-moments]</a>, these imply $$\label{eq:support-field-norms}
 \|\mathcal V_a\|_{L^2(\Pi_m)}
 +\|\operatorname{div}\mathcal V_a\|_{L^2(\Pi_m)}
 \le C l_a^{(m)}.$$

<a id="eq:support-ibp"></a>
Each rotation $x_j\partial_a-x_a\partial_j$ preserves the square-sum constraint. Its derivative of the Gaussian log density is $-\delta_{aj}x_ax_j$, so the corresponding derivative along $\mathcal V_a$ is exactly $-x_a$. Applying <a href="#eq:source-23" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:source-23">[eq:source-23]</a> with the coefficients defining $\mathcal V_a$ therefore gives $$\label{eq:support-ibp}
 \Pi_m[x_af]=\Pi_m[\mathcal V_a f]
                   +\Pi_m[f\operatorname{div}\mathcal V_a]$$ for smooth cylinder tests. To justify the singular coefficients, multiply them first by $\chi(R_0/\varepsilon)$, where $\chi$ is smooth, zero below $1$, and one above $2$. Direct calculation gives $$\mathcal V_aR_0
  =-2x_a\sum_{j=1}^J\frac{x_j^2}{R_0\delta_{aj}},\qquad
 |\mathcal V_a\chi(R_0/\varepsilon)|
  \le C l_a^{(m)}\frac{|x_a|}{R_0}
           \mathbf 1_{\{R_0\le2\varepsilon\}}.$$ The last expression tends to zero in $L^2(\Pi_m)$ by <a href="#eq:support-coordinate-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-coordinate-moments">[eq:support-coordinate-moments]</a> and <a href="#eq:support-inverse-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-inverse-moments">[eq:support-inverse-moments]</a>. At infinity we use a radial cutoff in a finite coordinate set containing $1,\ldots,J,a$ and all test coordinates. Every rotation in $\mathcal V_a$ preserves this radius, so differentiating that cutoff produces no error. This proves <a href="#eq:support-ibp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-ibp">[eq:support-ibp]</a> on the cylinder core, which then extends the identity to the form domain.

<a id="eq:support-resolvent-tail"></a>
Fix $z>0$ and a finite set $I\subset\{J+1,J+2,\ldots\}$. Applying <a href="#eq:support-ibp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-ibp">[eq:support-ibp]</a> to a vector of tests $(f_a)_{a\in I}$ and using Cauchy–Schwarz and <a href="#eq:support-field-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-field-norms">[eq:support-field-norms]</a> yields $$\left|\sum_{a\in I}\Pi_m[x_af_a]\right|
 \le C_z\left(\sum_{a>J}(l_a^{(m)})^2\right)^{1/2}
       \left(\sum_{a\in I}
          \{z\|f_a\|_2^2+c_*\mathcal E_m(f_a)\}\right)^{1/2}.$$ By the resolvent variational formula, followed by monotone convergence over finite $I$, $$\label{eq:support-resolvent-tail}
 \sum_{a>J}\langle x_a,(z+c_*H_m)^{-1}x_a\rangle_{\Pi_m}
       \le C_z\sum_{a>J}(l_a^{(m)})^2\longrightarrow0.$$ For every $s_0>0$ the scalar inequality $e^{-s\lambda}\le C_{s_0,z}(z+\lambda)^{-1}$ holds for $s\ge s_0$ and $\lambda\ge0$. Hence <a href="#eq:support-resolvent-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-resolvent-tail">[eq:support-resolvent-tail]</a> also makes the semigroup trace beyond $J$ tend to zero uniformly on compact subsets of $(0,\infty)$.

We have proved $\mathcal A_{g^{(m)}}\to1$ in $C_{\rm loc}((0,\infty))$. Each approximating function belongs to $K$, so closedness of $K$ proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "A spectral cap, shown near the upper edge. The posterior interaction $W_{p}$ retains eigenvalues below $2 − p^{2}$ and flattens those above it. The removed amount $(λ−(2−p^{2}))_{+}$ is the precision of the Gaussian observation in that eigendirection. The drawing is schematic; its geometry illustrates the defining spectral functions, not an eigenvalue density.",
      "figure_id": "fig:spectral-cap",
      "pdf_page": 12,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:spectral-cap",
          "exact_quote": "A spectral cap, shown near the upper edge. The posterior interaction\n$W_p$ retains eigenvalues below $2-p^2$ and flattens those above it.\nThe removed amount $(\\lambda-(2-p^2))_+$ is the precision of the Gaussian\nobservation in that eigendir",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.65cm,y=1.2cm,>=Stealth,font=\\small]\n \\fill[blue!8] (2,2)--(3.7,3.7)--(3.7,2)--cycle;\n \\draw[->] (0,0)--(4.25,0) node[right] {$\\lambda$};\n \\draw[->] (0,0)--(0,4.05) node[above] {eigenvalue};\n \\draw[gray,dashed,thick] (0.35,0.35)--(3.85,3.85);\n \\draw[blue!60!black,very thick] (0.35,0.35)--(2,2)--(3.9,2);\n \\draw[dotted] (2,0)--(2,2);\n \\draw[dotted] (0,2)--(2,2);\n \\node[below] at (2,0) {$2-p^2$};\n \\node[left] at (0,2) {$2-p^2$};\n \\draw (3,0.045)--(3,-0.045) node[below] {$2$};\n \\node[gray,rotate=36,above] at (1.1,1.1) {original eigenvalue};\n \\node[blue!60!black,below] at (3.15,1.9) {$\\min(\\lambda,2-p^2)$};\n \\draw[<->,thick] (3.5,2.06)--(3.5,3.45);\n \\node[right,align=left] at (3.57,2.8)\n   {observation\\\\precision};\n \\draw[<->] (2,-0.65)--(3.95,-0.65);\n \\node[below,align=center] at (2.98,-0.65)\n   {active eigendirections\\\\$\\lambda>2-p^2$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A 2D Cartesian plot with the horizontal axis labeled $\\lambda$ and vertical axis labeled \"eigenvalue\". The vertical axis has a marked value $2 - p^2$. The horizontal axis has marked values $2 - p^2$ and $2$. A diagonal line labeled \"original eigenvalue\" starts from the origin and continues upward with slope 1, becoming dashed for $\\lambda > 2 - p^2$. A solid blue path labeled \"$\\min(\\lambda, 2 - p^2)$\" follows this diagonal up to the point $(2 - p^2, 2 - p^2)$, then flattens into a horizontal line at height $2 - p^2$. Vertical dashed lines connect $(2 - p^2, 2 - p^2)$ to $2 - p^2$ on the $\\lambda$-axis and the dashed diagonal at $\\lambda = 2$ to $2$ on the $\\lambda$-axis. The region above the horizontal blue line and below the dashed diagonal line is shaded light purple. A vertical double-headed arrow spanning this shaded vertical gap is labeled \"observation precision\". Below the $\\lambda$-axis, a horizontal double-headed arrow extending rightward from $2 - p^2$ is labeled \"active eigendirections\" and \"$\\lambda > 2 - p^2$\"."
    }
  ],
